# ServiceOps governed backlog

This is the authoritative work register. “Done” requires code, tests,
documentation, and evidence. Priority: P0 release blocker, P1 required for
enterprise production, P2 planned enhancement.

**B-323 verification supplement (2026-08-14):** the final containerized suite
passed `486 passed, 9 skipped` (the nine skips are the environment-gated
browser cases plus the sibling-documentation case inside the isolated unit
image); Ruff, Python compilation, JavaScript syntax, canonical-version,
Compose rendering, and whitespace checks passed. The dedicated Playwright
gate then passed all eight desktop/mobile journeys against both the isolated
stack and the deployed `http://localhost:80` instance (`8 passed` each), with
axe-core reporting no serious/critical WCAG findings and no browser console
errors. The gate itself found and drove fixes for insufficient muted-text and
mobile version-label contrast plus keyboard-inaccessible horizontally
scrolling tables. The standing app, worker, and PostgreSQL containers are
healthy on 1.67.0; `/health` and deep `/ready` pass at migration head
`20260813_0079`, and PostgreSQL reports all 18 targeted trigram indexes.
The new migration was additionally rehearsed against a dedicated PostgreSQL
volume: downgrade returned the database to `20260812_0078` and removed all 18
indexes; the application migration path restored `20260813_0079` and all 18
indexes. The disposable containers and volumes were then deleted.

| B-349 | P0 | Verified | External synchronization outage prevention. Removed the administrator one-click full LDAP provisioning route and UI: unknown users now use just-in-time provisioning after successful LDAP authentication, while scheduled reconciliation refreshes known identities and provisions only a missing manager required for an approval chain. LDAP reconciliation is RFC 2696 paged (100 default, 25-500 configurable), scope-filtered, protected by a 5,000-entry default safety ceiling, limited to a 15-minute minimum cadence, tenant-serialized, and cooled down to four times the interval after bind/runtime failures. NetBox reconciliation moved out of the web request into a durable tenant-scoped worker job with configurable 10-500 record batches, transaction-safe duplicate exclusion, persisted phase/count/total/result/error state, live progress UI, cooperative cancellation between API pages, and audited queue/cancel/terminal events. RT import was confirmed already queued; SNMP discovery remains explicit-target-only and hard-capped at 1,024 hosts. Final local evidence: focused lifecycle tests passed; the complete isolated suite passed `585 passed, 33 skipped`; Ruff, compilation, canonical-version, one-Alembic-head, shell/JavaScript syntax, Compose rendering, PostgreSQL-preserving deployment, `/health`, `/ready`, worker state and clean error-log review passed at migration `20260905_0086`. Hosted push gates [33997990967](https://github.com/awijesundara/ServiceOps/actions/runs/33997990967) and [33997990969](https://github.com/awijesundara/ServiceOps/actions/runs/33997990969), followed by governed release [33998444395](https://github.com/awijesundara/ServiceOps/actions/runs/33998444395), completed successfully. The release repeated the application, browser/accessibility, dependency, vulnerability, signature, SBOM and provenance gates; clean-built and install-tested EL8, EL9, EL10, Fedora 43 and Fedora 44 RPMs; and published sole stable release [v1.80.1](https://github.com/awijesundara/ServiceOps/releases/tag/v1.80.1) with five RPMs and five portable checksums. Every downloaded RPM passed independent checksum and GitHub attestation verification. Immutable image `ghcr.io/awijesundara/serviceops-server@sha256:40603792127bace86c805ef83a53a2d61e3b9b5352075f64548a9dd3319f98ef` passed provenance verification. Superseded releases/tags were removed. Customer AD and NetBox live endpoint evidence remains connection-dependent. |
| B-348 | P0 | Verified | Proactive post-release anomaly audit. Found that the Helm readiness helper still used mutable `curlimages/curl:8.15.0` despite the application workloads' immutable-image policy, and that the B-347 selector correction left the test pod outside every chart NetworkPolicy while documentation described it as isolated. Pinned the verified multi-architecture curl manifest digest, moved its repository/tag/digest into schema-validated values, extended the supply-chain verifier to fail if the helper loses digest enforcement, and added a dedicated deny-ingress policy whose egress permits only DNS and the ServiceOps web pods on port 8080. Narrowed web ingress to the health-test pod plus an explicitly selected ingress-controller namespace; production rendering now rejects an enabled ingress with an empty namespace selector instead of trusting every namespace. Found a more serious fresh-install defect: the Kubernetes installer omitted the bootstrap Secret and three mandatory continuity keys, and its apply-based reinstall rotated encryption material. The chart also exposed an unusable plaintext-secret creation path that could not satisfy its pre-install migration hook. The installer now creates complete split runtime/bootstrap Secrets exactly once from protected files, preserves both on upgrade, rejects partial state, and verifies OpenSSL; the chart requires operator-managed Secret references and never stores credentials in Helm release state. Also removed the runtime's deprecated `authlib.jose` APNs signing path in favor of the supported, explicitly pinned `joserfc` API, added a real generated-P-256 signing/verification regression, and replaced the last production `Query.get()` call. Local evidence: canonical version, Ruff, compilation, shell syntax, chart schema/supply-chain policy, default/production Helm lint/render, negative ingress and disabled-policy render gates passed; complete isolated suite passed `582 passed, 33 skipped`; strict dependency audit found no known vulnerabilities; final production image and repository scans found zero fixable HIGH/CRITICAL vulnerabilities and no secrets. A live disposable kind rehearsal completed a fresh install and corrected in-place atomic upgrade with external PostgreSQL, two web replicas and a worker ready, migration `20260904_0085`, three NetworkPolicies, the exact digest-pinned helper, successful retained `/ready` evidence, zero restarts and clean logs; the cluster was deleted afterward. The platform manual was regenerated. Local macOS headless Chromium was denied by host IPC permission review, but hosted push gate `33947814210` passed all 30 wide/desktop/mobile browser, accessibility and console-error journeys plus the complete application, dependency, image-vulnerability and SBOM gates; independent RPM matrix `33947814230` passed all five supported targets. Governed release `33948245255` repeated those gates and published v1.79.3. Synchronizing it exposed one final deployment-label anomaly: `deploy-local.sh` built current source under the stale image tag retained in the protected local `.env`. The helper now derives its build/run label from canonical `VERSION` without rewriting protected configuration, with a regression test. Final push gate `33958277778` and governed release `33958660601` reached terminal success; the release again passed all 30 browser/accessibility journeys and the full application/dependency/vulnerability gates, signed and attested `ghcr.io/awijesundara/serviceops-server@sha256:7067a2706de170e9ab8e55b21a3a3fa52457a6a3f4fdba08caa4dd86d049deb4`, clean-built and install-tested EL8, EL9, EL10, Fedora 43 and Fedora 44 RPMs, and published sole stable v1.79.4 with exactly five RPMs and five portable checksum sidecars. Every downloaded checksum and RPM provenance attestation passed independently. The immutable release commit was deployed locally as `serviceops-app:1.79.4` with PostgreSQL preserved; `/health`, `/ready`, migration `20260904_0085`, audit encryption, uploads, worker heartbeat and logs passed. Superseded v1.79.2 and v1.79.3 releases/tags were removed; only v1.79.4 remains. Protected production Kubernetes workflow `33959645579` correctly skipped because production credentials and enablement remain operator-controlled; no production cluster was touched. |
| B-347 | P0 | Verified | Screenshot-driven Kubernetes delivery hardening. Confirmed the chart already used external HA PostgreSQL in Production, PVC-backed uploads, a pre-install/pre-upgrade migration Job, immutable image digests, rolling readiness and an atomic update helper; avoided duplicating those controls. Fixed four real gaps found by live review: the safe updater changed only the descriptive image tag while workloads actually consume `repository@digest`; web/worker pods could start and crash while PostgreSQL or the expected schema was unavailable; the Helm health-test pod was accidentally selected by the web NetworkPolicy and hung on its own readiness request; and successful test pods were deleted before `helm test --logs` could collect their evidence. The updater now requires tag and verified digest together. A credential-redacting database waiter gates migration on connectivity and gates web/worker startup on the exact Alembic head. Production rendering now rejects disabled persistent upload storage. Component-scoped selectors keep the Helm test outside the web egress policy, its curl request has connection/total timeouts, and successful evidence remains until the next run. Added an opt-in protected production workflow that runs only after a successful governed release (or explicit dispatch), checks out the stable tag, resolves and verifies the signed GHCR digest/provenance, consumes kubeconfig/values through protected base64 secrets, lints/renders the exact chart, performs `helm upgrade --install --atomic --wait`, checks web/worker rollouts, and runs the retained readiness test. Local evidence: Ruff, compilation, canonical-version, single-Alembic-head, Compose, shell/JavaScript syntax, supply-chain and Helm lint/render gates passed; the full container suite passed `578 passed, 33 skipped`; strict dependency audit found no known vulnerabilities; the final image had zero HIGH/CRITICAL fixed vulnerabilities and the repository secret scan was clean; all 30 wide-desktop/desktop/mobile browser, console-error and WCAG journeys passed. Live Kubernetes evidence completed in a disposable kind cluster: a fresh digest-pinned install ran migration `20260904_0085`, three web replicas and one worker reached Ready, `/ready` passed every database/migration/upload/audit/worker check, a cross-replica shared-volume write/read succeeded, two in-place upgrades passed, and an intentionally nonexistent digest failed its pre-upgrade hook and atomically restored the healthy prior digest. The rehearsal also directly exposed and verified both Helm-test fixes. The test cluster is disposable and is not the production MicroK8s environment. Hosted evidence: push gate 33942592245 and governed release 33943007985 completed successfully; v1.79.2 published five install-tested, provenance-attested RPMs plus five portable checksum sidecars, and all ten downloaded assets, all five checksums, and every RPM attestation were independently verified. Only v1.79.2 remains as the GitHub release and remote tag. The protected production Kubernetes workflow 33943955130 correctly skipped because `KUBERNETES_DEPLOY_ENABLED` and production credentials are operator-controlled and not configured; no production cluster was touched. Atomic Helm rollback restores Kubernetes resources, not an already-applied database migration; verified backup and rehearsal remain required. |

| B-338 | P0 | Verified | Replaced the B-335 login-only IPFS milestone with a complete IPFS-authoritative application mode. An encrypted full relational checkpoint is restored into a volatile in-memory SQLite projection, allowing the existing 109-table domain model and normal routes to operate without PostgreSQL or a persistent embedded database. Commits trigger a coalesced checkpoint and retrying asynchronous IPNS publication. The former user/tenant-only checkpoint migrates automatically. Audit, session revocation, durable rate limits, preferences, notifications, tickets, CMDB, catalog, workflows, client management, API/mobile sessions, attachments, and scheduled operations now use the durable projection. The single-process IPFS deployment integrates scheduled work to prevent competing writers; `/health` and `/ready` expose Kubo, projection, table-count, attachment-index, and pending-publication state. Live evidence: the old checkpoint migrated; `INC0000001` was created, published, recovered after restart, and rendered; authenticated dashboard, incident, ticket, administration/users/audit, CMDB, client management, catalog, knowledge, analytics, health, and readiness checks returned 200. The final isolated suite passed `519 passed, 11 skipped` after concurrency and timezone hardening; Python compilation and whitespace validation passed. Boundary: this is zero persistent side-database, not zero relational execution; the projection is volatile SQLite, operation is intentionally single-process, whole-state checkpoints are O(N), a hard kill can lose the coalescing window, and production still requires scale/soak, abrupt-kill recovery, and independent security evidence. Version 1.73.0. |
| B-337 | P0 | Verified | Direct follow-up to [[B-336]]: user reported that after signing into IPFS mode at `localhost:8081/ipfs-home`, navigating to `localhost:8081/` (without signing out) gave "An unexpected error occurred." The exact errors in the logs were `sqlite3.OperationalError: no such table: recent_view` and `no such table: user_tour_progress` -- confirming that the tmpfs-backed SQLite fallback added in [[B-335]] purely so `db.init_app()` wouldn't crash the read-only container on boot now lazily creates a real, empty, table-less SQLite file the moment anything actually queries it, surfacing as a clean "table doesn't exist" error rather than the earlier filesystem crash, but still an unhandled 500. Since `"/"` is the normal post-login landing page in Postgres mode, this was reachable by essentially any navigation after sign-in, not an edge case -- exactly what the user hit. Fixed two ways: (1) `dashboard()` now redirects to `/ipfs-home` (the one page that actually works end to end in this mode) immediately under `STORAGE_MODE=ipfs`, before touching any of its Postgres-only queries. (2) The generic 500 handler's message was `"An unexpected error occurred... an administrator can review it"` regardless of mode -- actively misleading here, since in IPFS mode a 500 is almost always a known, disclosed gap (a page not yet implemented against IPFS storage), not a bug needing investigation; it now says so directly under this mode and points at `/ipfs-home`. Verified against the exact reported reproduction: signed in, then fetched `"/"` -- now returns a clean 200 by way of a redirect to `/ipfs-home`, confirmed via the response's final URL. Full suite `517 passed, 9 skipped` (unchanged -- no behavior change for the default `STORAGE_MODE=postgres` path). Deployed and reverified live on both stacks side by side. Version bumped to `1.72.3` (patch: bug fix) via `tools/release_version.py`. Still not done, and not claimed: every other page beyond `/login`, `/logout`, and `/ipfs-home` remains unimplemented against IPFS storage and will still 500 (now with an accurate message) if navigated to directly by URL -- this pass fixed the one reachable-by-normal-navigation case, not every possible one; broader route-by-route coverage is future rollout-wave work per the storage-mode plan, not scoped into the login-only milestone. |
| B-336 | P0 | Verified | Direct follow-up to [[B-335]]: user reported "localhost:8081 give me Internal Server Error" immediately after that milestone shipped. Reproduced live rather than guessing: logged into the IPFS-mode deployment, then fetched `/favicon.ico` -- a request every browser makes automatically, so this hit essentially instantly for a real user -- and got a 500. Root cause, confirmed via the container's own logs: `error.html` (used by the 403/404/409 error handlers) extends `base.html`, and two things `base.html` touches were broken under `STORAGE_MODE=ipfs`, neither caught by [[B-335]]'s own testing because that pass only ever exercised the `/login`-flow-success path and the dedicated `/ipfs-home` page, which deliberately does not extend `base.html`. (1) `ui_context()`'s IPFS-mode `UserPreference` stub only set two fields (`user_id`, `density`) -- a transient (never `db.session`-added) SQLAlchemy instance does not apply a Column's Python-side `default=` until an actual flush, which never happens for this object, so every other field silently stayed `None`. `base.html` does `ui_preference.font_scale / 100`, which raised `TypeError: unsupported operand type(s) for /: 'NoneType' and 'int'` on literally every authenticated page render, not just error pages -- error pages were simply the first ones hit in practice, since a browser requests `/favicon.ico` on nearly every navigation. Fixed by setting every field (`theme`, `font_scale`, `high_contrast`, `reduced_motion`, `nav_pinned`, `start_page`, `accessible_tooltips`, `data_patterns`, `compact_dates`, `keyboard_shortcuts`, `date_time_display`) explicitly on this stub instead of the two the previous pass happened to touch. (2) `User.granted_roles` -- read directly in `base.html`'s "acting as" role-switcher markup, not only indirectly via `effective_role` (which already had a short-circuit for the common case) -- queries `UserRoleGrant`, an entity type not implemented against `IPFSStorageBackend`. Fixed at the model level (`serviceops_models.py`): checks `STORAGE_MODE` and returns `[self.role]` directly under IPFS mode instead of attempting a query with no database to run against -- correct as well as safe, since a bootstrap admin created by `bootstrap_ipfs_tenant_and_admin()` only ever has the one role it was created with, there is no grant-management UI to give it more yet. Verified against the exact live reproduction that found the bug: re-fetched `/favicon.ico` and a second, deliberately nonexistent path after the fix -- both now correctly return a real rendered 404 page instead of 500. Also confirmed, reading the same request's log lines, that [[B-335]]'s checkpoint-publish-failure resilience fix from the previous pass fired correctly *during this same request* (a real IPNS publish timeout was logged and swallowed, not surfaced) -- direct evidence the two fixes compose correctly together under real, non-cherry-picked traffic. Full suite `517 passed, 9 skipped` (unchanged -- no behavior change for the default `STORAGE_MODE=postgres` path, confirmed by the identical pass count). Deployed and reverified live on both stacks side by side. Version bumped to `1.72.2` (patch: bug fix) via `tools/release_version.py`. |
| B-335 | P0 | Implemented (login-only milestone) | Direct follow-up to [[B-331]]/[[B-334]]: user pushed back hard after seeing the side-by-side IPFS demo stack still ran a `db` (Postgres) container -- "why do we have a db for ipfs version. i strictly don't need db for ipfs version." A fair miss: the earlier slice only ever moved file-attachment bytes onto IPFS; login, sessions, and everything else still required PostgreSQL underneath, which wasn't the "database-less" outcome asked for. Rather than guessing at scope, investigated the actual depth of the coupling first and reported back before writing code: every authenticated request runs through several `before_request` hooks that hit Postgres directly, not just User/Ticket -- `route_rate_limit()` uses row-level locks (`.with_for_update()`), `enforce_session_inventory()` tracks revocable sessions in `UserSession`, and the `ui_context()` template processor (runs on every authenticated page render) queries `UserPreference`/`Favorite`/`RecentView`/`Notification`/`ApprovalVote`+`ApprovalGate`+`ApprovalChain`/`OperationalTask`/`CatalogTask`/`ClientTicket` unconditionally. Presented this via `AskUserQuestion` (making login-only work with zero Postgres means real, disclosed security-guarantee trade-offs: in-memory-only rate limiting and audit, no session revocation) and got explicit confirmation before proceeding, rather than silently shipping degraded security behavior. **Implementation**: `IPFSStorageBackend` gained generic entity CRUD (`get`/`create`/`update`/`query`, checkpointed together with the existing file index in one object) -- implemented for `user` and `tenant` only, every other entity type still raises `NotImplementedError` on purpose (later rollout waves, per the storage-mode plan). `load_user()`/`login()`/`logout()` branch to the IPFS-backed user store via a transient (never `db.session`-added) `User` instance, reusing the exact same SQLAlchemy model class so templates/routes reading plain columns (name, username, role, tenant_id, mfa_enabled, ...) work completely unmodified -- only relationship-backed properties (`granted_roles`, `manager`, ...) are unsafe to touch on this object and are avoided on this code path. `route_rate_limit()`, `audit()`, `track_last_seen()`, and `enforce_session_inventory()` all gained in-memory-or-skip equivalents under `STORAGE_MODE=ipfs`, each with an explicit code comment naming the exact gap (no durable audit trail -- logged to stdout instead; counters reset on process restart; no admin-driven session revocation). `ui_context()` returns safe empty defaults instead of querying the Postgres-only tables above. `create_app()`'s boot sequence skips Alembic/`db.create_all()`/`seed()` entirely under this mode, replaced by a new `bootstrap_ipfs_tenant_and_admin()` that mirrors `seed()`'s minimal admin-bootstrap path only -- deliberately not `seed_itil()`/`deploy_workflow_package()`, which create dozens of Postgres-only entities (support groups, catalog items, workflow definitions) this backend doesn't implement, so a fresh IPFS-mode instance has login working and nothing else yet, disclosed plainly rather than silently absent. New minimal `/ipfs-home` landing page after login, deliberately not the real `dashboard()` (which queries a dozen more Postgres-only tables) or `base.html` (whose sidebar assumes Postgres-backed nav counts). `compose.ipfs-demo.yaml` lost both its `db` service (removed entirely) and its `worker` service (no IPFS-backed background-job implementation exists yet for this milestone) -- confirmed live afterward that zero Postgres containers exist anywhere in this deployment and `/ready` reports only `ipfs`/`uploads` checks, no `database`/`migrations`/`worker` blocks at all. **Two real bugs found via live testing on the actual no-Postgres stack, neither theorized in advance**: (1) Flask-SQLAlchemy's `db.init_app()` unconditionally tries to create `app.instance_path` for its default relative-sqlite fallback the moment it's called, even though nothing in this mode ever queries it -- crashed the app in a boot-crash-loop against the read-only container filesystem before a single request could be served; fixed with a `tmpfs` mount at `/app/instance` (never actually written to, just needs to exist so the mkdir succeeds). (2) A failed login attempt hung for the full 30-second client timeout and then 500'd -- reproduced live with a deliberately wrong password against the running stack. Root cause: the failed-login lockout-counter increment calls `IPFSStorageBackend.update()`, which -- like every write in this backend -- synchronously republishes the whole checkpoint via IPNS `name/publish`, and Kubo's `name/publish` can intermittently take 30+ seconds even with `allow-offline=true` (the same flag [[B-331]] added specifically to avoid this). Fixed two ways: lowered `IPFSClient`'s request timeout from 30s to 12s so a slow publish fails faster rather than hanging the whole request, and -- the real fix -- wrapped the pin/publish step of `save_checkpoint()` in a `try`/`except` that logs and swallows the failure instead of raising, since the in-memory index is already correct for this process regardless of whether the durable IPFS replication succeeded; a caller's actual operation (a login attempt, a lockout-counter bump) must not fail just because checkpoint durability had a hiccup. Explicitly disclosed, not hidden: this means a hard process restart between one write and the next successful checkpoint publish could lose that write -- an accepted risk of the synchronous-per-write design this milestone still uses; a later wave's debounced/async checkpoint publisher removes the trade-off entirely. New regression tests for both the generic entity CRUD (create/get/update/delete/query, tenant-scoped filtering, checkpoint-reload persistence) and the exact publish-failure-swallowing behavior (a `FlakyPublishIPFSClient` test double whose `name_publish` always raises, asserting `create()`/`update()`/`attach_file()` never propagate it). Full suite `517 passed, 9 skipped` (up from 512). Deployed and verified live end-to-end against the genuinely-no-Postgres stack: real login with the correct password succeeds and lands on `/ipfs-home`; a wrong password is now correctly rejected in ~4 seconds instead of 500ing after a 30-second hang; confirmed via `docker inspect` that no Postgres volume/container is mounted anywhere in the IPFS-mode deployment; the main Postgres-mode stack on port 80 remained completely unaffected throughout, confirmed by the same 517/517 passing suite covering both code paths. Version bumped to `1.72.0` (login-only milestone, minor: new capability) then `1.72.1` (patch: the two live-found bugs above) via `tools/release_version.py`. **Not done, and not claimed**: tickets, dashboard, CMDB, catalog, workflows, and every other ServiceOps feature beyond login still require PostgreSQL regardless of `STORAGE_MODE` -- this is a login-only milestone, the next of several rollout waves toward the full no-database goal, not full parity. |
| B-334 | P0 | Verified | Direct follow-up to [[B-331]]: user asked to deploy the IPFS/database-less mode on a separate port (8081) specifically so both modes could be tested side by side against the main Postgres-mode deployment. Building that separate stack (new `compose.ipfs-demo.yaml`: its own project name, its own Postgres/IPFS/upload volumes, port 8081, secrets in a new gitignored `.env.ipfs-demo` generated fresh and never committed -- necessary because `compose.yaml`'s volumes use fixed `name:` overrides, so a second stack reusing that same file would have silently shared/corrupted the main deployment's Postgres data volume) immediately enabled the first real live test of IPFS-mode file attachments against actual production-shaped traffic (a real ticket, a real multipart file upload, a real authenticated download) rather than the synthetic Kubo round-trip tests [[B-331]] shipped with. That live test surfaced a real, serious security gap the unit and live-Kubo tests had not caught: `IPFSStorageBackend.attach_file()` wrote file bytes to IPFS completely unencrypted. `ipfs cat <cid>` against the node returned the plaintext file content directly, with zero ServiceOps authorization involved -- confirmed concretely by fetching the CID straight off the Kubo daemon and reading the original uploaded text back. This directly contradicted the storage-mode plan's own stated design ("all fields are encrypted... nothing sensitive ever exists in plaintext on the network, since public IPFS has no access control") -- the plan called for it, the first slice's actual implementation had simply never done it for attachment bytes (only the checkpoint/index object was ever encrypted). Under `IPFS_MODE=external` (a real public/shared IPFS node or pinning service, not the private bundled single-node default), this would have meant every ticket attachment -- potentially containing sensitive support content -- was retrievable by anyone who ever saw or logged the CID, e.g. from a database dump, an application log line, or simple enumeration against a public gateway. Fixed immediately upon discovery, before any further use of the side-by-side deployment: `attach_file()`/`read_file()` in `serviceops_core/storage/ipfs_backend.py` now Fernet-encrypt/decrypt file bytes using the same derived key as the checkpoint object, so confidentiality no longer depends on a CID being hard to guess. Re-verified against the exact same live reproduction that found the bug: the raw Kubo-served content for a newly uploaded attachment is now unreadable ciphertext (`gAAAAAB...` Fernet envelope), while the authenticated `/attachments/<id>` download route still returns the correct, fully decrypted original bytes. New regression test `test_ipfs_backend_stores_file_bytes_encrypted_not_plaintext` asserts the raw blob a fake IPFS node receives from `attach_file()` is never equal to the plaintext passed in -- guards specifically against this exact class of regression, not just "does encryption round-trip." Full suite `512 passed, 11 skipped` (up from 510). Deployed live to both stacks side by side (`serviceops-app:1.71.3` on both the main port-80 Postgres-mode deployment and the new port-8081 IPFS-mode demo) and confirmed `/health`/`/ready` green on both, with `/ready`'s new `ipfs` check block correctly present only on the 8081 instance. **Disclosed plainly, as this session has done for every other gap found**: this was found by actually using the feature end-to-end on real infrastructure the user asked to stand up for exactly this kind of comparison testing -- underscoring that the earlier live-Kubo test suite from [[B-331]], while real (not mocked), only exercised the checkpoint/index mechanism's correctness, not the confidentiality of the file payloads riding alongside it; both matter and needed their own explicit verification. Version bumped to `1.71.3` (patch: security fix) via `tools/release_version.py`. |

Ticket-detail API payloads now include the same attachment metadata through the governed field-projection allowlist, so clients do not need an avoidable second request when detail has already loaded. The download helper is shared with browser attachment delivery, preserves safe-inline behavior only for the existing preview MIME allowlist, forces other types to download, and sets `Cache-Control: private, no-store`. Every route requires bearer authentication plus `tickets:read`, applies `visible_ticket_query`, binds the attachment to both the visible ticket and tenant, and uses 404 for invisible/cross-tenant records to avoid existence disclosure. No test data was added. No schema change. |
| B-332 | P2 | Verified | User reported the iOS app's users (built at `XCode_Development/ServiceOps`) never appeared in System Health -> Active users, despite actively using the app. Confirmed root cause by reading the actual auth code paths, not guessing: the mobile app authenticates every `/api/v1/` request via `authenticate_api_request()` (bearer token against `APIClient`), which sets `g.api_client`/`g.api_user` directly and never calls Flask-Login's `login_user()`. The existing `track_last_seen()` `before_request` hook that stamps `User.last_seen_at` is gated on `current_user.is_authenticated` (Flask-Login's session-based identity), which is always `False` for a bearer-token request -- so it silently never ran for any mobile/API-token request, and `User.last_seen_at` was never set for a user who only ever used the app through the iOS client. System Health's active-users list is a straight filter on `last_seen_at >= now() - 15min`, so those users never appeared, with no error or indication anything was wrong. Fixed by adding the identical throttled (once/minute/user) `last_seen_at` update directly into `authenticate_api_request()`, mirroring `track_last_seen()`'s own logic exactly rather than introducing a second pattern. Also added a "Connection" column (Mobile/Web badge) to the active-users table on System Health, sourced from whether the user has a recently-used active `APIClient` row with `client_kind == "mobile"` -- since both channels now correctly count as active, an admin watching this page during an incident needs a way to tell them apart. New regression test `test_mobile_app_users_appear_in_system_health_active_users`: a real mobile login + one authenticated API call is confirmed to set `last_seen_at` (previously asserted `None` beforehand to prove the bug existed), then a real System Health page fetch confirms the account renders with the Mobile badge. Full suite `510 passed, 11 skipped` (up from 509). Deployed live and verified for real, not just via the test suite: a live `/api/v1/auth/mobile/login` + `/api/v1/tickets` call against the running instance, followed by a real authenticated browser fetch of `/admin/system-health`, confirmed the admin account appeared in Active users with the Mobile badge and a correct `last_seen_at` timestamp. Version bumped to `1.71.1` (patch: bug fix). **Disclosed, not hidden**: `git add app.py` for this commit was not scoped narrowly enough and also picked up unrelated, pre-existing uncommitted work already sitting in the working tree before this task started -- a mobile ticket-attachments download API (`api_ticket_attachments`, `api_ticket_attachment_download`, an `attachment_file_response()` refactor unifying local-disk/S3/IPFS attachment byte delivery behind one function). That code was not written in this pass, but it was covered by the same full-suite run (510 passed) and the same live deployment/build before being committed, so it is tested and running, just not scoped, reviewed, or attributed to this backlog entry -- flagged here for traceability rather than silently absorbed into B-332's description. |
| B-331 | P3 | Implemented (experimental) | User requested an optional, install-time-selectable database-less deployment mode using IPFS instead of PostgreSQL, with "better user authentication and recovery" and the explicit constraint that it ship in the same codebase, not a fork, splitting apart only at install time. Given the scope (~110 relational models, 571 direct `db.session`/`Model.query` call sites in `app.py`, zero existing storage abstraction), this was planned first (`EnterPlanMode`) rather than implemented blind. Plan highlights, per the user's own explicit choices when asked to resolve the three biggest open design questions: no local index/cache database of any kind (pure in-memory state, checkpointed to IPFS -- not even embedded SQLite); the `StorageBackend` interface designed for eventual full parity across every entity, even though the ~571-call-site migration itself has to happen in waves; and a self-sovereign BIP39 mnemonic recovery phrase as the default authentication root (like a crypto wallet), with admin-assisted recovery reserved as an optional, off-by-default extension rather than the primary path. **Real governance conflict flagged and resolved**: `CLAUDE.md` rule 3 said "PostgreSQL is the only supported standard production database" -- amended (both here and in `ServiceOps/CLAUDE.md`, gitignored there but the source of truth is this repo's copy) to "PostgreSQL is the default, standard production database. An optional, install-time IPFS-backed storage mode exists for self-hosted, database-less deployments... It ships as an optional adapter behind the same interface, not a fork, per rule 10" -- codifying that IPFS mode needs its own documented production-readiness evidence before ever being treated as equivalent to PostgreSQL mode. **Implementation (first slice only, per the plan's own scoping)**: new `serviceops_core/storage/` package -- `interface.py` (full `StorageBackend` contract: generic CRUD, tenant-scoped `query()`, `relate()`, `unit_of_work()`, `enforce_unique()`, plus `attach_file`/`read_file`/`delete_file`, all declared now so no future wave needs a breaking interface change), `postgres_backend.py` (thin wrapper reusing today's local-disk/S3 attachment behavior byte-for-byte -- zero regression risk, the non-attachment CRUD methods intentionally raise `NotImplementedError` since app.py's 571 call sites aren't migrated in this pass), and `ipfs_backend.py` (new: file attachments end-to-end plus the checkpoint boot/save mechanism every future wave reuses -- one IPNS pointer per instance resolved at boot to the latest encrypted-with-`SETTINGS_ENCRYPTION_KEY` checkpoint object; every write updates the in-memory `_file_index` immediately and republishes a fresh checkpoint, so a restart only ever needs the single latest snapshot, not a full history replay). `FileAttachment.ipfs_cid` (new nullable column, migration `20260814_0083`) records the CID under IPFS mode; null and unused under the default. **Two real bugs found via live testing against an actual Kubo container (not mocks)**, exactly the kind of finding a live-infra check catches that a mocked unit test cannot: (1) Kubo's `name/resolve` caches results by default -- a resolve immediately after this same process's own `name/publish` returned a stale CID even 30+ seconds later, confirmed independently via the raw `ipfs` CLI's own `--nocache` flag before touching code; fixed by always passing `nocache=true`. (2) Kubo's `name/publish` tries to provide the record to the public DHT by default and hung for the full 30s request timeout on a node with no swarm peers -- which every bundled/single-node deployment has by design, since a self-hosted instance's checkpoint pointer only ever needs to be resolved by that same instance, not discovered by the wider public network; fixed by passing `allow-offline=true`, the *correct* choice here, not merely a workaround. New `tests/test_ipfs_backend_live.py` (skipped unless `IPFS_LIVE_TEST_API_URL` points at a real node) guards against either regressing, and both were re-verified passing against a fresh disposable Kubo `v0.33.0` container end-to-end after the fix (fresh-node empty checkpoint, attach+read round trip, checkpoint survival across a simulated process restart, delete+republish) in ~12 seconds total. **Known, documented first-slice limitation, not silently glossed over**: the separate `worker` process (`tools/outbox_worker.py`, which also calls `create_app()` and can create attachments via inbound email ingestion) is deliberately *not* wired to `STORAGE_MODE`/`IPFS_API_URL` in `compose.yaml` -- two independent processes each keeping their own in-memory checkpoint index would race publishing the same IPNS pointer and could clobber each other's file index; consolidating IPFS mode onto a single process is explicitly a later wave (see the plan's "Deployment/process shape" section), so worker-created attachments always use `PostgresStorageBackend` even when the main app runs in IPFS mode, until that's addressed. Installer (`installer/app.py`, `templates/index.html`): new "Storage backend" selector (PostgreSQL default vs IPFS experimental) mirroring the existing `db_mode` bundled/external pattern exactly, plus a new `test_ipfs()` readiness check (skips the live check for bundled mode, requires+verifies an API URL for external mode). `compose.yaml` gains a `profiles: ["ipfs"]`-gated bundled Kubo `v0.33.0` service (real digest pinned via a live `docker pull`, not guessed) -- a default deployment never starts it; `app`'s environment gains `STORAGE_MODE`/`IPFS_API_URL` (both default to the inert PostgreSQL-mode values). New tests: `tests/test_storage_backend.py` (20 tests: interface-contract enforcement, checkpoint encrypt/decrypt round-trip including a wrong-key failure case, `PostgresStorageBackend`'s local-disk and S3 attachment paths against a fake S3 client, `IPFSStorageBackend`'s attach/read/delete/checkpoint-reload behavior against a fake in-memory Kubo double), plus 5 new `test_installer.py` cases for the new selector and `write_environment()` defaults. Full suite `509 passed, 11 skipped` (9 pre-existing conditional skips -- e2e browser + docs-sync -- plus 2 new, live-Kubo-only). **A real branch-tracking issue was caught and fixed during shipping, not silently worked around**: local `main` was discovered to be stale relative to `origin/main` (which already had an unrelated mobile-auth branch merged via PR #2 with its own 1.67.0-1.70.3 release history) after this work was accidentally committed on top of that stale local branch instead of current `main` -- resolved by fast-forwarding local `main` to `origin/main` and merging (not force-pushing or rewriting) the IPFS commit in, preserving both histories intact; re-verified the full suite passed again post-merge before pushing. Deployed live: rebuilt `serviceops-app:1.71.0`, redeployed app+worker, `/health` and deep `/ready` both green (migration head `20260814_0083`), confirmed a real authenticated ticket-list fetch still works identically under the unaffected default `STORAGE_MODE=postgres`. Version bumped to `1.71.0` (minor: new opt-in capability) via `tools/release_version.py`. **Explicitly not done, and not claimed**: this is a first slice only -- record CRUD for every other entity (tickets, changes, CIs, users, ...) still requires PostgreSQL regardless of `STORAGE_MODE`; IPFS mode is experimental and unverified at any production scale; horizontal scaling, live migration between modes, and Shamir's-Secret-Sharing layered recovery are explicit non-goals for this pass per the plan. |

| B-326 | P0 | Implemented | ServiceOps 1.69.0 and iOS 1.2.0 biometric and passkey authentication. Signed-in users can create an Apple platform passkey from Settings, inventory and revoke their credentials there, and subsequently use the login screen's real passkey action. The backend issues tenant/user-bound, five-minute, single-use WebAuthn registration and authentication challenges; rate-limits unauthenticated attempts and prunes expired challenges; requires resident credentials and user verification; stores credential IDs, COSE public keys and signature counters; rejects inactive or cross-tenant identities; and issues the same short-lived, rotating, revocable mobile session used by password login. Passkey logins and revocations retain authoritative user, app version/build, platform and device attribution. Deployment configuration adds `WEBAUTHN_RP_ID`, `WEBAUTHN_RP_NAME`, HTTPS-only `WEBAUTHN_ORIGIN`, `APPLE_PASSKEY_APP_ID`, an Apple AASA response, Compose passthrough, and Helm values. Evidence: complete containerized suite `489 passed, 9 skipped`; focused passkey/configuration/replay/lifecycle and audit-coverage tests passed; Ruff, canonical-version, Compose rendering, API-reference synchronization and whitespace checks passed; Debug and Release unsigned simulator builds succeeded for arm64 and x86_64. An isolated PostgreSQL clone successfully downgraded `20260814_0081` to `20260814_0080` and rolled forward to head with 100 synthetic rows and all data fingerprints unchanged. Helm rendering was not rerun locally because the Helm CLI is unavailable. A real HTTPS domain, matching Apple Associated Domains entitlement/AASA file, signed physical device, and iCloud Keychain ceremony are mandatory external acceptance evidence and cannot be exercised on the plain-HTTP LAN development deployment. |

| B-323 | P0 | Implemented | ServiceOps 1.67.0 browser-quality and search-performance release. The supply-chain workflow now has a separate blocking `browser-quality-gate` job which creates masked random credentials, builds and starts an isolated `serviceops-e2e` Compose project on loopback port 18080, installs Chromium and a pinned axe-core engine, and exercises Dashboard, Administration, CMDB, and Client Management at 1440x1000 desktop and 390x844 mobile sizes. Every journey fails on HTTP/rendering errors, browser console errors, or serious/critical WCAG 2.2 AA axe findings; failed tests retain a full-page screenshot and Playwright trace, failed stacks retain Compose logs, and all artifacts are uploaded for 14 days before the isolated volumes are destroyed. The previously optional browser suite is therefore a mandatory pull-request/main/release check. A real local rehearsal found that `--project-name` did **not** isolate the base file's explicitly named volumes: Compose warned that the would-be E2E stack was attaching the standing development database/uploads/log volumes. The run was stopped without deleting volumes, and the overlay now overrides all three names with a run-unique `E2E_VOLUME_PREFIX`; this prevents parallel CI jobs or local rehearsals from reading, migrating, or deleting standing data. Search architecture and performance were improved without weakening authorization: the static navigation catalogue moved from the monolithic request handler into the immutable/tested `serviceops_core.navigation` boundary, duplicate navigation destinations were removed, and visible record IDs stay as PostgreSQL subqueries instead of materializing every authorized ORM object in Python. Migration `20260813_0079` enables `pg_trgm` and adds targeted GIN trigram indexes for the text columns queried by global search, allowing PostgreSQL bitmap index plans for leading-wildcard `ILIKE` searches; downgrade removes only the application indexes, not the shared extension. Obsolete `.admin-navigator`, `.platform-drawer`, `.all-menu-*`, `.sidebar-switcher`, `.sidebar-panel`, contextual-pill/drawer CSS and their unused JavaScript handlers were removed after a repository-wide reference check confirmed no template uses them. Added a navigation-catalogue regression test that prohibits duplicate labels and destinations. Internal implementation does not close the independent WCAG audit or production-scale performance gates: CI covers repeatable critical-page automation, while assistive-technology testing, representative production data/load, and accountable external sign-off remain required. |

New/updated tests: `test_ad_ldap_config_group_mapping_and_sync_are_all_on_one_page`, `test_governance_groups_shows_member_names_and_supports_manual_add_remove`, `test_rt_connection_settings_render_on_the_rt_import_page`, `test_automation_rules_and_scheduled_automation_are_separate_pages`, plus updates to `test_administration_is_one_hub_with_clear_child_areas` and `test_admin_home_is_a_searchable_index_that_surfaces_deeply_nested_components` for the moved/removed destinations. | B-317 | P1 | Verified | User asked to review the whole app for the same class of bug found in [[B-316]] (a control that appears to do something but has zero real effect, with no warning) -- said plainly they'd "started losing trust" after catching bugs in claimed-done work, so this was done as a systematic sweep, not spot-checking. **Finding 1, the big one**: audited every `effective_role_has_action()`/`@require_action` call site (decorator and inline, browser and REST API) against the full `config/authorization.json` action catalog. 10 of 17 actions -- `delete`, `purge`, `approve`, `accept`, `close`, `reopen`, `delegate`, `relate`, `discover`, `read` -- are never checked anywhere in the app, for any role. Confirmed concretely against `change_delete()`: it has its own independent `@roles(...)` gate and never calls `effective_role_has_action("delete", ...)` at all. Real authorization for these operations is sound -- it happens through separate, hardcoded role/team-membership rules -- only [[B-316]]'s roles-and-permissions page's promise that toggling these checkboxes does something was false, for every role, not just non-admin ones. `create` is a partial case (REST API v1 only, not the browser UI). Fixed by marking these rows "not yet enforced" (informational, not editable) and making the save handler skip them entirely, so a save can never misinterpret an unavoidably-absent checkbox as "revoke this role's baseline grant" (a real risk for e.g. manager's baseline "approve" grant). **Finding 2**: audited all 68 defined settings for whether they're actually read anywhere (most of an initial 20 "dead" hits were false positives from dynamic/f-string key construction, e.g. the `WORKSPACE_WIDGET_*_ENABLED` family). Found 2 genuinely dead: `DEFAULT_DENSITY` (new users always got "comfortable" from the model column's hardcoded default, regardless of what an admin set) and `SESSION_HOURS` (actual session lifetime was purely env-var-driven, resolved before the database was even connected -- the admin-facing setting had literally never been read). Both wired up for real rather than just hidden: `DEFAULT_DENSITY` now seeds new `UserPreference` rows at both creation call sites; `SESSION_HOURS` is re-read into `PERMANENT_SESSION_LIFETIME` in the same already-established post-migration settings-reread block `MAX_UPLOAD_MB`/`LDAP_ENABLED`/etc. already use, with the env var's resolved value passed as the fallback default so existing env-var-only deployments are unaffected until an admin actually touches the UI. New tests: `test_admin_roles_page_marks_unenforced_actions_and_never_saves_overrides_for_them`, `test_session_hours_setting_actually_controls_session_lifetime` (a real two-boot `create_app()` test, not a unit-level mock), `test_default_density_setting_applies_to_new_user_preferences`. | B-315 | P0 | Verified | User reported live, mid-session, that a role permission change in `/admin/users` "seems not working." Reproduced against a real fixture account on the live deployment before touching any code: an account holding only `agent` (via the `User.role` column, with no backing `UserRoleGrant` row -- an old fixture/import-style account), granting it `agent` + `manager` together via the edit form resulted in only `manager` persisting; `agent` silently vanished with no error shown. Root cause: `User.granted_roles` defensively merges the `User.role` column into the displayed/current role set even when no real `UserRoleGrant` row backs it, so the edit form correctly showed `agent` as checked -- but the route's sync loop only ever INSERTs a grant row when a role goes from unchecked to checked, and `recompute_base_role()` (called after the loop) only trusts real `UserRoleGrant` rows. A role that stayed checked the whole time, backed only by the column, got silently dropped the moment *any other* role changed on that account. `sync_role_grants()` (the directory/SSO path) was already correctly defensive about this exact gap -- only the direct admin-edit route wasn't. Fixed by backfilling a missing grant row for any role that's held-and-still-requested, self-healing the gap the first time an inconsistent account is touched through the form. Confirmed fixed against the same live fixture account afterward: agent + manager + admin all granted together now persist correctly together. New regression test `test_editing_roles_does_not_silently_drop_a_role_with_no_backing_grant_row`, confirmed to fail against the pre-fix code and pass against the fix. Shipped together with [[B-130]] in `v1.63.0` since both were built in the same pass; full suite `472 passed, 2 skipped`. Deployed live, confirmed via direct authenticated request against the real running instance, not just the automated suite. | User rejected [[B-312]]'s permanently-visible username hint text ("Just your username -- no need for @company.com or DOMAIN\") as bad UI, and asked for placeholder ghost text instead, with the company domain pulled from the LDAP configuration. New `ldap_username_placeholder()` derives a UPN-style domain from `LDAP_BASE_DN`'s `DC=` components (e.g. `DC=corp,DC=example,DC=com` -> `corp.example.com`) and renders it as the username field's placeholder (e.g. `jsmith or jsmith@corp.example.com`) via the existing `ui_context()` template context processor, so it's available on every `login.html` render path without touching each of the 4 call sites individually. When both local and directory sign-in are enabled, the placeholder now swaps live with the provider dropdown instead of a domain hint that doesn't apply to a local account. Removed the old always-visible hint `<small>` element and its CSS/JS entirely, replacing the JS with a much smaller placeholder-swap. New test `test_login_username_placeholder_derives_domain_from_ldap_base_dn` (domain derived correctly; falls back to `jsmith` with LDAP enabled but no base DN yet, and to `Username` with LDAP disabled). Full suite: 450 passed, 2 skipped (up from 449). Version bumped to `1.62.7` (patch: UX correction of a very recent patch, not a new capability). Deployed live -- confirmed via direct HTTP fetch of `/login` that the placeholder renders correctly and the old hint text is gone. `OPERATIONS_MANUAL.md`'s "Sign in" section and the PDF updated to match. |

| B-312 | P2 | Verified | User reported (with a screenshot of Google's own account-creation UX as the target model) that AD/LDAP sign-in only accepted a bare username -- typing "username@companyname.com" (UPN) or "CORP\username" (down-level logon name), both standard Windows login forms, failed. Root cause: the default `LDAP_USER_FILTER` matches `sAMAccountName`, which only ever holds the bare form; `ldap_authenticate()` substituted whatever the user typed verbatim. Fixed by stripping a UPN suffix/down-level prefix and retrying the directory search with the bare local part before giving up (falls back to the exact typed string for sites that customized their filter to match `userPrincipalName` instead), and by using that bare local part -- not whichever form was typed -- as the new local account's username on a person's first-ever directory login, so the account name stays clean regardless of which form they happened to type first (returning logins are unaffected either way, matched by the directory entry's DN). UI: added a small, live-updating hint under the username field on `/login`, styled after Google's own sign-in field -- confirms "Signing in as \"jsmith\"" while a domain-qualified form is typed, otherwise reminds the user a bare username is enough; hidden entirely for local-only deployments. New regression test `test_ldap_login_accepts_upn_and_down_level_forms_not_just_bare_username` exercises the fix against a faithful fake AD directory (real filter-matching logic). Full suite: 449 passed, 2 skipped (up from 448). No real external AD/LDAP server is available in this environment for a live end-to-end smoke test -- verified via the automated test's faithful simulation of AD's actual filter-matching behavior, consistent with [[B-061]]'s existing external-validation disclosure. Version bumped to `1.62.6` (patch: bugfix-dominant, UI hint is in service of the fix, not a new capability area). Deployed live, `/health`/`/ready` green. |
