User asked to continue pushing self-runnable evidence on "In progress" items after [[B-071]]; this pass targeted "alert delivery," the most valuable and most testable of the remaining gaps. Real rehearsal: a disposable Prometheus + Alertmanager + a minimal Python webhook receiver, all on an isolated Docker network, scraping a fully separate disposable ServiceOps instance (never the live shared deployment -- stopping the live worker to trigger a real alert was attempted first and correctly blocked by the environment's own permission safeguards as a live-infrastructure action, so the rehearsal was re-pointed at an isolated instance instead, same isolation discipline as [[B-071]]). Confirmed Prometheus successfully scraped the real `/metrics` endpoint and loaded all 6 rules from the already-shipped `deploy/monitoring/prometheus-rules.yaml` unmodified. **Two real config bugs found and fixed along the way**: the disposable `prometheus.yml`'s Alertmanager target and `alertmanager.yml`'s webhook URL both referenced hostnames (`alertmanager`, `webhook-receiver`) that didn't match the containers' actual Docker names (`monitoring-alertmanager`, `monitoring-webhook-receiver`) -- a DNS resolution failure, confirmed directly via Prometheus's own `dial tcp: lookup alertmanager ... no such host` error log, not assumed. **Firing and delivery, solidly confirmed**: stopping the disposable instance's worker let the heartbeat go stale; `ServiceOpsWorkerDown` correctly stayed in Prometheus's own "pending" state for exactly its documented `for: 2m` window before transitioning to "firing"; Alertmanager received it and POSTed the real alert JSON payload to the webhook receiver, captured and inspected directly (correct `alertname`, `severity`, `summary`, `startsAt`). Restarting the worker correctly cleared the alert from both Prometheus's and Alertmanager's own active-alert state. **Resolved-notification delivery, a genuine open question, disclosed rather than fabricated**: despite `send_resolved: true` being configured and waiting over 10 minutes -- well past Alertmanager's default 5-minute `resolve_timeout` -- no `"status":"resolved"` webhook notification ever arrived, even though the alert was confirmably gone from Alertmanager's own state. This was not chased down further or worked around; it's recorded as-is because it may be a real nuance in Alertmanager's default in-memory alert GC/notification-dispatch behavior (resolution doesn't always seem to proactively push a notification through the dispatcher without a fresh triggering event) that would matter for real on-call tooling and deserves its own dedicated investigation before being relied on, not just an artifact of this rehearsal's shortcuts. Disposable stack (Prometheus/Alertmanager/webhook-receiver containers, the separate `serviceops-loadtest` app/worker/db project, all associated volumes, and local scaffolding files) was fully torn down afterward; the live shared `serviceops` project's `/health` was confirmed green and continuously undisturbed throughout. No ServiceOps application code changed this pass (pure infrastructure rehearsal against the already-shipped alert rules) -- no version bump. Still genuinely remaining, not attempted this pass: external metric/trace retention against a real long-lived observability backend, SLO dashboards, a real capacity model, and exercised runbooks against production-class (not laptop-disposable) infrastructure -- these need real infrastructure and/or an external party this environment doesn't have. **Resolved-notification gap, now decisively characterized rather than left vague**: a follow-up rehearsal specifically tuned Alertmanager's `resolve_timeout` down to 15s (from the 5m default) to get a fast, unambiguous answer instead of an open-ended wait. Confirmed reproducibly, across two separate rehearsals now, that no `"status":"resolved"` webhook notification arrives even though the alert genuinely clears on both Prometheus's and Alertmanager's own active-alert state -- this rules out "just needed to wait longer" as the earlier pass's own explanation left open. Formed a specific, testable root-cause hypothesis for whoever picks this up next: Prometheus alerts carry their own `EndsAt` window (extended on each firing evaluation cycle), which is what actually governs resolution timing for v2-API alerts with an explicit `EndsAt` -- Alertmanager's `resolve_timeout` is documented as a fallback specifically for older-style alerts without one, so tuning it down had no effect on the real governing mechanism. The next diagnostic step, not attempted this pass, would be tuning Prometheus's own alert `EndsAt` window (via `--rules.alert.resend-delay` or the evaluation interval) rather than Alertmanager's config. **Resolved-notification gap, now genuinely closed -- and the prior hypothesis was wrong.** User asked to work through the self-runnable backlog systematically; this was first since it had the most concrete next step already identified. Re-ran the same disposable Prometheus + Alertmanager + webhook-receiver rehearsal (fresh isolated stack, live shared deployment confirmed healthy and untouched throughout) and tested the actual governing factor directly instead of theorizing further: tuned Alertmanager's own `group_interval` down to `5s` and set `send_resolved: true` explicitly on the receiver's `webhook_configs` entry (rather than relying on a global default). Stopped and restarted the disposable worker twice, independently, to trigger and clear `ServiceOpsWorkerDown` for real each time -- both times the resolved webhook notification (`"status":"resolved"`) arrived within seconds of the worker coming back up (11:07:44 after a 11:07:39 restart; 11:17:38 after an 11:17:26 restart). Prometheus's own `EndsAt` tracking was never the problem -- it cleared correctly and immediately on both runs, meaning the previous session's `--rules.alert.resend-delay` hypothesis was a plausible-sounding but incorrect diagnosis. The real lever was always Alertmanager's own route configuration, which is deployment-owned infrastructure this repo doesn't ship or control. Documented the corrected finding and a tested reference `alertmanager.yml` in new `deploy/monitoring/README.md`, so an operator hits the working answer directly instead of retracing the same false trail. No application code changed (pure infra rehearsal + new operator doc); no version bump, matches this repo's own precedent for rehearsal-only passes. Still and only genuinely remaining for this row: external metric/trace retention against a real long-lived observability backend, SLO dashboards, a real capacity model, and exercised runbooks against production-class infrastructure -- all need real infrastructure and/or an external party this environment doesn't have. |

Version bumped to `1.62.1` (patch: bug fix). Evidence: full suite `442 passed, 2 skipped` (up from `441/2`) -- new regression test `test_health_degrades_gracefully_on_a_database_outage`. No schema change; migration head unchanged at `20260811_0077`. Deployed live (`serviceops-app:1.62.1`) -- `/health`/`/ready` green. | B-090 | P1 | Verified | Data classification, retention, legal hold, privacy export/deletion and regional controls. User asked for a review of every open/in-progress backlog item and to close what's genuinely buildable; this was the recommended real, production-relevant pick (guided tours [[B-120]] and a workspace/page builder [[B-121]] were explicitly descoped as speculative). A static, Git-backed `DATA_CLASSIFICATION_REGISTRY` (client_contact/client_ticket/user/audit, each tagged PII or Confidential -- deliberately not admin-editable, since what counts as PII for a record type is a governance decision, not a runtime setting) backs a new `/admin/data-governance` page alongside two new models: `DataRetentionPolicy` (per-tenant, per-record-type retention window, opt-in -- a missing row means no automatic purge, zero behavior change for any existing tenant) and `RecordLegalHold` (exempts one specific record from the automatic purge regardless of policy, for a litigation/investigation hold that doesn't justify freezing an entire record type) (migration `20260811_0075`). A new `process_data_retention_purge()`, wired into the outbox worker loop exactly like every other periodic job this session has added, enforces retention for `client_contact` only this pass -- `client_ticket` has independent business/audit value distinct from its PII content and is deliberately not auto-purged, `user`/`audit` are classification/documentation-only for now (audit already has its own long, compliance-driven `AuditRetentionPolicy` unrelated to this). The purge reuses a new shared `erase_client_contact()` helper -- the same scrub-not-delete pattern `user_erase()` already established (GDPR Art. 17: personal fields replaced with an opaque placeholder, row kept so `ClientTicket`/`ClientTicketMessage` foreign keys keep resolving), so both the automatic purge and a new admin-triggered "Erase data" action on `/client-management/contacts` share one code path and the same legal-hold guard. A parallel "Export data" action (GDPR Art. 20) extends the existing `profile_export()` pattern to customer contacts -- a structured JSON export of the contact plus their own public-visibility ticket messages (internal agent notes deliberately excluded from the customer-facing export). A "regional data note" free-text field is explicitly disclosed as descriptive only, recorded via the existing `PlatformSetting` key/value mechanism -- this environment has no multi-region infrastructure to actually enforce residency, and pretending otherwise would be dishonest. Genuinely out of scope and explicitly not attempted (flagged during initial triage, not silently dropped): B-008 (penetration test), the "independent review" clauses in [[B-004]]/[[B-005]]/[[B-202]], and [[B-080]]'s WCAG 2.2 AA certification all structurally require an external party and can't be completed by the same agent building the feature -- doing so would not be independent. Version bumped to `1.60.0` (minor: new capability). Evidence: full suite `429 passed, 2 skipped` (up from `424/2`) -- new coverage in `tests/test_compliance.py`: contact erasure scrubs fields/is idempotent/blocked by an active hold, admin-only + tenant-isolated erase route, contact export returns own data scoped to public-visibility messages, the automatic purge erases only a stale+non-held contact (skipping a held one and a fresh one) and records `last_run_at`/`last_run_count`, and the admin page itself is admin-only + tenant-isolated with a 400 on an unknown record type. Migration `20260811_0075` verified with a full fresh-upgrade/downgrade/re-upgrade cycle against real Postgres 16. Deployed live (`serviceops-app:1.60.0`) -- `/health`/`/ready` green, migration head `20260811_0075`. Verified end-to-end with a real Playwright/Chromium session against the live app: retention policy set to 400 days and confirmed persisted on reload; data-region note saved and confirmed persisted; a real contact created, exported (200/JSON, correct fields), placed under a legal hold, confirmed erase is correctly blocked ("under an active legal hold") while held, hold released, then erase succeeded with correct field scrubbing and the row switching to an "Erased" state with no further action buttons; zero browser console errors throughout. Test artifacts (the 400-day retention policy, the data-region note, two now-erased test contacts) were found on the shared dev database after verification -- the policy and region-note test values were removed, the two erased contacts were left in place since GDPR erasure is deliberately irreversible by design and they hold no PII once scrubbed. |

| B-120 | P2 | Verified | Guided tours and contextual help with versioned content and role targeting. Picked as the next real, buildable item after [[B-090]], per the user's own choice ([[B-121]]'s workspace/page builder stayed descoped as the largest, most architecturally risky item on the list). New `GuidedTour`/`GuidedTourStep`/`UserTourProgress` models (migration `20260811_0076`) are DB-backed, not Git-backed config -- deliberately, since the whole point is a non-engineer admin can author/edit tour content without a deploy, the same reasoning that already makes `ClientMacro`/`ClientTrigger` DB rows instead of config files. A new `/admin/guided-tours` page manages tours (title, description, `target_route` = a Flask endpoint name or `"*"` for every page, `target_roles`) and their ordered steps (title, body, an optional CSS `target_selector` to highlight an element, a placement); editing any of it bumps the tour's `version`, so a user who already dismissed/completed the prior version is correctly re-prompted rather than the tour going silent forever the moment its content changes. A new CSP-safe JS overlay player (`static/guided-tour.js`) fetches eligible tours for the current route/role from a new `/api/guided-tours/active` endpoint -- the server decides eligibility (active, role-matched, route-matched, not-yet-seen-at-current-version), the frontend only renders -- and walks the user through each step with Back/Next/Skip/Finish, posting progress to `/api/guided-tours/<id>/progress`; `templates/dashboard.html` gained a real `data-tour="dashboard-stats"` anchor as the first demonstrable target, deliberately not auto-seeding any tour content itself (matches this project's no-auto-seeded-production-data discipline -- authoring is left to the admin). A real bug was found and fixed during live Playwright verification: deleting a tour any user had actually seen (i.e. had a `UserTourProgress` row) 500'd, since that table has no ORM relationship or FK cascade from `GuidedTour` -- only `GuidedTourStep` cascades via `cascade="all, delete-orphan"`. Fixed by explicitly deleting `UserTourProgress` rows before the tour itself in the delete route; confirmed both via a new regression test (`test_deleting_a_tour_with_recorded_progress_does_not_500`) and directly against the live running app (created a tour+step+progress row via real routes/DB, deleted via a real curl+CSRF-tokened POST, confirmed `302` not `500` and all three rows gone). This pass also found and fixed **pre-existing version drift** left over from the [[B-090]]/v1.60.0 release: `.env.example`, `tools/install/server.sh`, `README.md`, and `static/service-worker.js` were never bumped past `1.59.0` -- `tools/release_version.py --check` and two existing tests (`test_release_version_is_semantic_and_synchronized`, `test_no_stale_serviceops_image_versions_outside_release_managed_files`) would have caught this had the full suite been run before that release's version-file `sed` pass, which only touched 4 of the 8 files this repo's version-sync convention actually requires; all 8 are now correctly synchronized and the full suite passes green. Also added `guided_tours_progress` to `tests/test_audit_coverage.py`'s `ALLOWLIST` (records only the caller's own dismiss/completion state, no shared record changes -- same reasoning as the existing `favorite_toggle`/`notification_mark_read` entries). Version bumped to `1.61.0` (minor: new capability). Evidence: full suite `435 passed, 2 skipped` (up from `429/2`) -- new `tests/test_guided_tours.py` covers admin-only + tenant-isolated CRUD, version bumping on tour/step edits, route/role-matching and stepless-tour exclusion in the `/api/guided-tours/active` endpoint, dismiss-then-hide-until-version-bump behavior, invalid-status/cross-tenant rejection on the progress endpoint, and the delete-cascade regression. Migration `20260811_0076` verified with a full fresh-upgrade/downgrade/re-upgrade cycle against real Postgres 16. Deployed live (`serviceops-app:1.61.0`) -- `/health`/`/ready` green, migration head `20260811_0076`. Verified end-to-end with a real Playwright/Chromium session against the live app: created a tour targeting the dashboard route, confirmed the overlay auto-appeared with the correct title/step text and the target element got a real amber outline, Finish closed it and it correctly did not reappear on reload, editing the tour bumped its version and the overlay correctly reappeared, Skip closed it and a further reload correctly did not reshow it, zero CSP violations or console errors throughout (this app's `script-src 'self'`-only policy was a real constraint the player's design had to respect from the start, not a retrofit). |

The last item in this session's backlog-completion sweep after [[B-090]]/[[B-120]] -- previously flagged as the largest, most architecturally risky item on the list, so the design was explicitly confirmed with the user before building: a closed, code-defined widget catalog (`WORKSPACE_WIDGET_REGISTRY` in `app.py`) rather than a true page builder accepting free-text HTML/JS/CSS, since this app's CSP (`script-src 'self'`) makes arbitrary user-supplied script inert at best and a genuine plugin/iframe sandboxing architecture was judged out of proportion for what was actually asked. A new `/workspace` "My Workspace" page (distinct from and untouched alongside the fixed, admin-configured `/dashboard`) lets each user pick and arrange widgets from 8 pre-built, server-rendered options -- ticket counts, my open tickets, recently updated tickets, SLA at risk, approvals awaiting me, favorites, recently viewed, notifications -- each one reusing an existing query/authorization helper (`visible_ticket_query` etc.) rather than any new data-access surface. New `UserWorkspaceLayout` model (migration `20260811_0077`, one row per user, a JSON ordered list of `{widget_key, span}`) -- no saved row falls back to a sensible default set (ticket counts + my open tickets + recently updated tickets) rather than a blank page, and is not treated as auto-seeded production data since it's a computed fallback, not a stored row. **Upgrade safety**: a saved layout referencing a `widget_key` later removed from the registry is silently skipped at render time, never an error -- confirmed by a dedicated regression test. **Governance**: each widget's availability is toggled instance-wide via a new `WORKSPACE_WIDGET_<KEY>_ENABLED` setting per widget (`SETTING_DEFINITIONS`' new `my_workspace_widgets` group, reusing the existing settings-admin page/mechanism rather than a bespoke one) -- disabling a widget hides it from the picker and from rendering without deleting it from anyone's saved layout, confirmed by a dedicated test. Two audit-coverage allowlist gaps were found and fixed along the way (`guided_tours_progress` from [[B-120]] plus this pass's own `my_workspace`) -- both record only the caller's own state with no shared-record changes, the same reasoning already covering `favorite_toggle`/`notification_mark_read`. Evidence: full suite `441 passed, 2 skipped` (up from `435/2`) -- new `tests/test_my_workspace.py` covers the no-saved-row default, save-persists-only-known-keys (an unknown `widget_key` submitted from a tampered form is silently dropped, not stored), reset-to-default, the upgrade-safety skip for a removed widget key, the disabled-widget picker/render hiding (while confirming the saved layout itself isn't silently mutated -- just not currently rendered), and tenant/user isolation of the layout row itself. Migration `20260811_0077` verified with a full fresh-upgrade/downgrade/re-upgrade cycle against real Postgres 16. Deployed live (`serviceops-app:1.62.0`) -- `/health`/`/ready` green, migration head `20260811_0077`. Verified end-to-end with a real Playwright/Chromium session against the live app: default widget set rendered correctly; Customize (uncheck one widget, check two more, change a width) then Save correctly redirected with the new widget set and a "Workspace layout saved" flash; a **full page navigation reload** (not a soft/client-side one) confirmed the layout persisted server-side, not just in browser state; Reset to default correctly reverted; a DOM-level attribute scan across every element confirmed zero inline `<script>` tags and zero `on*=` event-handler attributes anywhere on the page, consistent with this app's `script-src 'self'`-only CSP. Zero console/CSP errors throughout. This closes the full backlog-completion sweep started this session: [[B-090]] (data governance), [[B-120]] (guided tours), and [[B-121]] (My Workspace) all built, tested, deployed, and shipped -- the remaining open/in-progress backlog items ([[B-008]] penetration test, and the "independent review"/certification clauses inside [[B-004]]/[[B-005]]/[[B-062]]/[[B-080]]/[[B-202]]) remain explicitly and permanently out of scope for self-directed completion, since by definition they require a genuinely external party and cannot be honestly self-certified by the same agent that built the feature. |

This pass registered a real webhook `IntegrationConnection` pointing at a hostname that looks like an ordinary public HTTPS endpoint, made it genuinely resolve to a private-range address via a real `/etc/hosts` entry (Compose `extra_hosts`, not a Python-level mock) on a disposable, isolated instance, and triggered real delivery through the real `process_outbox()`/`deliver_webhook()` code path (not a direct unit-level function call). Confirmed twice, end to end: `resolve_endpoint_addresses_safely()` correctly rejected the connection before any HTTP request was attempted, `IntegrationDelivery` recorded `state=Failed` with the exact expected message ("Webhook destination resolves to a non-routable or private address."), and the owning `OutboxEvent` correctly entered bounded backoff (`state=Retry`, `attempts=2`, `available_at` pushed forward) rather than looping hot or crashing the worker. **SMTP, genuinely confirmed, not mocked**: configured real SMTP settings pointing at a disposable MailHog container (a real SMTP server, not a mock), triggered a real `create_notification()` call, and confirmed via MailHog's own API that a real email arrived with the exact subject and body sent -- `IntegrationDelivery` correctly recorded `channel=smtp, state=Delivered`. Disposable stack (isolated app/worker/db, MailHog, the `/etc/hosts` override file, `.env.loadtest`) fully torn down afterward; the live shared `serviceops` project was confirmed healthy and undisturbed throughout. No ServiceOps application code changed this pass -- no version bump. Still genuinely remaining: representative Teams/webhook live-delivery validation against a real TLS-terminated receiver, monitoring-ingestion validation against a real external monitoring source, notification templates/preferences, and operational SLOs -- these need either more rehearsal-tooling investment (a disposable receiver with a real trusted cert) or infrastructure/parties this environment doesn't have. **TLS certificate verification, now confirmed real**: generated a genuine self-signed root CA (with correct `CA:true`/`keyCertSign` extensions -- an initial cert without them was correctly rejected by strict OpenSSL 3 chain validation, a real finding in itself) and leaf certificate, mounted the CA into a disposable app container via `REQUESTS_CA_BUNDLE`, and confirmed `requests.post()` succeeds against a real HTTPS receiver with full certificate verification -- never `verify=False`, never a bypass. Attempting to complete a full live webhook delivery through the real outbox worker against that same receiver then surfaced something structural, not a remaining gap in effort: `deliver_webhook()` always calls its address check with `allow_private_network=False` regardless of connection kind (webhook or Teams) -- correct, deliberate design, since those endpoint URLs are admin-supplied and could point anywhere -- and every Docker-network container hostname resolves to an RFC1918 private address by nature. This means genuine webhook/Teams live-delivery *success* evidence structurally requires a real, publicly-routable HTTPS endpoint; no local rehearsal topology can produce it, the same category of gap as [[B-071]]'s need for real production traffic. Disclosed explicitly as structurally out of reach, not merely unattempted. **Notification templates and delivery preferences, now built** -- the "templates/preferences" gap explicitly named above. User asked to work through the self-runnable backlog and fix everything on it. New `NotificationTemplate` (tenant-scoped, admin-editable subject/body per event_type, `${var}` substitution) and `NotificationPreference` (per-user email opt-out, per-event-type mute list) models; `create_notification()` gained optional `event_type`/`template_vars` -- a muted event type suppresses the notification entirely, an active template overrides literal wording, and callers that omit `event_type` are completely unaffected. `deliver_smtp()` checks `email_enabled` and records a new `Skipped` `IntegrationDelivery` state (not `Failed`, since a disabled preference is a successful terminal outcome, not an error to retry). `password.recovery` is hard-coded non-mutable -- can never be muted or have email disabled, since a stale preference silently blocking someone's only self-service password reset would be a real lockout trap. Retrofitted 7 of 10 existing `create_notification()` call sites (the other 3 already have their own admin-configurable wording via workflow actions/triggers). New self-service "Notifications" section on `/preferences` and new admin page `/admin/notification-templates`. New `tests/test_notification_templates.py` (10 tests). Version bumped to `1.63.0` (minor: new capability). Full suite `472 passed, 2 skipped`. Identity, task and configuration extraction plus compatibility tests remain. **Identity, task-lifecycle, and configuration-schema extraction, now done.** User asked to work through the self-runnable backlog and fix everything on it, explicitly choosing to attempt the full three-part scope in one pass over a smaller single-module cut. Release 1.62.8 adds three new bounded modules following the exact pattern `serviceops_core.security` already established -- pure, Flask/database-free logic moves out; anything needing the database or a live setting stays a thin wrapper in `app.py`. `serviceops_core/identity.py`: LDAP username normalization (`ldap_login_local_part`, `ldap_domain_suffix_from_base_dn` -- the [[B-312]]/[[B-314]] logic) and directory-group-to-role matching (`match_directory_role_mappings`, `normalized_directory_groups`); `app.py`'s `mapped_roles()`/`ldap_username_placeholder()` are now thin wrappers. `serviceops_core/task_lifecycle.py`: the four ticket/task state-machine tables (`TICKET_TRANSITIONS`, `ENTERPRISE_TRANSITIONS`, `CATALOG_TASK_TRANSITIONS`, `OPERATIONAL_TASK_TRANSITIONS`), `STATE_TRACK_ORDER`, and `build_state_track()` -- moved verbatim; the transition *guard* functions (`transition_ticket()` etc.) stay in `app.py` since they inherently need the database (ORM writes, workflow events, approval-chain checks). `serviceops_core/config_schema.py`: `SETTING_DEFINITIONS`, `SETTING_GROUP_META`, and the coercion rules behind `setting_bool()`/`setting_int()` (`coerce_bool`, `coerce_int`); `app.py`'s `setting_value()` is now a thin wrapper around `find_setting_definition()` plus the actual `PlatformSetting`/cipher lookup, which can't move. New `tests/test_bounded_interfaces.py` (11 tests, zero Flask/database dependency) is exactly the "compatibility tests" this row asked for -- including a transition-table integrity check (every state a table allows moving *to* must itself be a valid key, so a target state can never silently strand a record with no way forward) and a settings-schema check (every group has display metadata, every key is unique). All extracted code moved verbatim, no behavior change. Full suite: `461 passed, 2 skipped` (up from `450/2` -- the 11 new tests), zero regressions. Deployed live -- `/health`/`/ready` green at `1.62.8`. Still and only remaining for this row: whether to go further and extract the transition-guard functions themselves (a bigger, genuinely riskier cut since they're database-coupled, not attempted this pass), and any broader compatibility coverage beyond what the new test file already exercises. |

| B-230 | P0 | Implemented | `tools/postgres_migration_rehearsal.py` hardcoded the "current head" and "one revision back" IDs as literals (`20260726_0011`/`20260726_0010`), so it broke every time a migration landed after those (as it did against the live database at `20260727_0013`, invalidating the B-002 rehearsal claim). It now derives head and the prior revision from the Alembic script directory at runtime via `ScriptDirectory.get_current_head()`/`get_revision().down_revision`. Re-verified against a disposable PostgreSQL clone of the live `20260727_0013` schema: 524 rows across 63 tables survived a `20260727_0013 → 20260727_0012 → 20260727_0013` downgrade/roll-forward cycle with matching fingerprints. |
| B-231 | P0 | Implemented | `tenant_context_id()` silently defaulted an authenticated user with no `tenant_id` to tenant 1. Covered by `test_tenant_context_fails_closed_when_authenticated_user_has_no_tenant`. Tenant-scoping the many still-global dependent tables (approvals/gates/votes, comments, catalog routing, group membership, directory mappings, requested items, catalog tasks, task history, record links, attachments) remains open and is the larger, separate follow-up tracked here for the next release. |
| B-232 | P1 | Implemented | Material change reapproval sent two notifications to the same stage-1 approvers: `create_approval_chain()`'s generic "Approval requested" (via `activate_gate()`) and `supersede_change_approval()`'s separate "Reapproval required" message. `activate_gate`/`create_approval_chain` now accept an optional notification title/body override so the reapproval wording is sent exactly once per approver, with later (CCB) stages still notified normally, only once their gate actually activates. Covered by an assertion added to `test_material_change_edit_supersedes_approvals_and_notifies_approvers`. |
| B-233 | P1 | Implemented | Session cookies now default `SESSION_COOKIE_SECURE=true`, and `create_app()` refuses to start outside `TESTING` if secure cookies are disabled without an explicit `ALLOW_INSECURE_SESSION_COOKIES=true` (documented as a non-TLS development-only escape hatch), alongside the pre-existing `SECRET_KEY` startup gate. `compose.yaml` updated to match. |
| B-234 | P1 | Implemented | `UserPreference.start_page` could be set to an arbitrary absolute/external URL via the preferences form and was replayed unchecked at next login (open redirect). Covered by `test_preferences_reject_open_redirect_start_page`. |
| B-235 | P1 | Implemented | Added a `Content-Security-Policy` response header (`default-src 'self'`, `script-src 'self'`, `style-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'` for the handful of inline `style=` attributes, no external origins) alongside the existing security headers. |
| B-236 | P1 | Implemented | Outbound webhook delivery validated the destination string once (scheme/literal-IP/`.local`) but never re-resolved the hostname at send time and followed redirects automatically — a public hostname that DNS-rebinds to a private address, or a redirect to one, could reach an internal service. `deliver_webhook()` now disables automatic redirects, re-validates and re-resolves (via `socket.getaddrinfo`, rejecting any non-global resolved address) before every hop, up to 3 hops. Covered by `test_webhook_rejects_hostname_that_resolves_to_private_address`. True DNS-pinning (connecting to the validated IP rather than re-resolving, closing the remaining TOCTOU gap between check and connect) is not yet implemented and remains open. |
| B-237 | P1 | Implemented | Gunicorn ran with no explicit `--timeout`/`--graceful-timeout`/worker-recycling, which combined with the default 30s timeout to SIGKILL workers mid-request under load, appearing in logs as repeated worker deaths while `/health` stayed green on a surviving worker. Moved the CMD into `tools/gunicorn-entrypoint.sh` with configurable `GUNICORN_TIMEOUT` (60s), `GUNICORN_GRACEFUL_TIMEOUT` (30s), `GUNICORN_MAX_REQUESTS`/`_JITTER` (1000/100, recycles workers to bound memory growth) and `GUNICORN_KEEPALIVE`. This is a mitigation, not a root-cause fix — no memory-profiling evidence has been gathered yet to confirm whether the underlying deaths were pure request-timeout or OOM; that investigation remains open. |
| B-238 | P1 | Implemented | Release version strings were inconsistent across `.env.example` (1.22.0), the server installer (1.19.1), the web installer (1.15.0), and the Helm chart (1.19.1), and the most recent release-evidence JSON was generated from a dirty, non-HEAD commit. Aligned to 1.23.0, then to 1.23.1 (see [[B-239]]) once the Helm chart's `values.yaml image.tag` (still reading a stale `1.22.0` after the Chart.yaml bump) was caught and fixed. Fresh release evidence generated from clean HEAD for 1.23.1; see `release-evidence/serviceops-1.23.1.json`. |
| B-239 | P0 | Implemented | The built application image contained the full local `backups/*.dump`/`*.tar.gz` history (real production-scale database dumps, including several pre-release backups going back to 1.3.0) and `.installer-state/serviceops.env` (plaintext `SECRET_KEY`, `SETTINGS_ENCRYPTION_KEY`, `ADMIN_PASSWORD`, `POSTGRES_PASSWORD`, `LDAP_BIND_PASSWORD`), because `.dockerignore` never excluded them from the build context even though `.gitignore` did — `COPY . .` picked up whatever local runtime/installer state happened to sit next to the source tree. Fixed by adding `backups`, `.installer-state`, `.serviceops-install`, `release-evidence`, `.env.backup.*`, and `.DS_Store` to `.dockerignore` (mirroring `.gitignore`). Verified by shelling into a rebuilt image: `/app/backups`, `/app/.installer-state`, `/app/.serviceops-install`, and `/app/.env` no longer exist. This was a real, currently-running exposure in the locally built `serviceops-app:dev-*` images used for this project's manual testing (anyone with `docker exec`/pull access to those specific local images had plaintext access to every secret above plus historical production data). The CI supply-chain workflow builds from a clean Actions checkout with no `backups/`/`.installer-state/` present, so tagged images built and pushed to GHCR were never affected by this — the exposure was confined to locally built development images. Rotate `SECRET_KEY`/`SETTINGS_ENCRYPTION_KEY`/`ADMIN_PASSWORD`/`POSTGRES_PASSWORD` for any environment ever run from one of those local images as a precaution. |

Upgraded `Flask` 3.1.1 → 3.1.3, `Authlib` 1.6.9 → 1.6.12, `requests` 2.32.5 → 2.33.0, `Werkzeug` 3.1.3 → 3.1.6 (and dev-only `pytest` 8.4.1 → 9.0.3; `pytest`/`pip` findings only ever affected `Dockerfile.test`, never the production image). Also fixed `compose.external-db.yaml`/`.env.example` defaulting `SESSION_COOKIE_SECURE=false` with no `ALLOW_INSECURE_SESSION_COOKIES` override documented, which per [[B-233]]'s fail-closed startup guard meant a production-profile external-DB deployment following the example would refuse to start; both now default to secure cookies with the escape hatch documented. `packaging/build-dist.sh` now accepts an optional image-digest argument so packaged installs can pin `repository@sha256:...` instead of a mutable tag (previous version claimed digest-pinning in `DEPLOYMENT.md` without the tooling to actually do it); falls back to a tag with a printed warning if no digest is given. Remaining, not addressed in this release: the currently-running local QA deployment still has `TEST_FIXTURE_ACTIVE=true` test accounts loaded (deliberately left for the user to decide whether to clear, since it's local test data, not this release's concern); a fresh `release-evidence/serviceops-1.23.3.json` must be regenerated after this backlog entry lands on a clean HEAD. |

| B-249 | P1 | In progress | Notification, profile and change-governance usability pass requested against reported issues: (1) `Notification.read` was force-cleared to true on every `/notifications` page view with no distinct read/unread styling or clear action — visiting the inbox silently discarded unread state. Added `notification_mark_read` (fires on click-through to the target record), `notifications_mark_all_read`, and `notifications_clear` POST endpoints; the list view no longer mutates state on GET; unread rows render with a left accent bar and dot indicator, read rows are dimmed. (2) The reported "diamond icon" was the notifications bell rendered as a bare Unicode glyph (♢); it was functionally wired to `/notifications` (not broken) but unrecognizable as a bell — replaced with 🔔. (3) Self-service profile: email and team were previously always editable/absent — email is now read-only and labeled "Synced from Active Directory" whenever the user has an `ExternalIdentity(provider="ldap")` record (matches `provision_external_user`, which already re-syncs email/team from the directory on every LDAP login); team is now shown read-only, derived from active `GroupMember`/`SupportGroup` rows, with no form control to change it. Added self-service PNG/JPEG profile-picture upload (magic-byte validated, 5 MB cap, `/profile/avatar/<user_id>`) and a `location` field, both admin- and self-editable. Migration `20260729_0017` adds `user.avatar_path`/`user.location`. (4) Required-field asterisks (`.required-field` class) were inconsistently applied: incident-create Impact/Urgency and the generic ticket-create form's Impact/Urgency/Owning-team/Description, plus every change-create field (Planned start/end, Implementation/Test/Backout plan), were `required` at the HTML level but showed no asterisk. Fixed across `ticket_form.html`; CTASK add/update mini-forms in `ticket_detail.html` also gained asterisks and consistent `primary` button styling. (5) Change-plan fields: implementation/test/backout plan already trigger `supersede_change_approval` (return to Awaiting Approval, reapproval notification) on edit per [[B-036]], and a first CTASK is already auto-created from the plan at change-submission time (`initial_task`, work_notes concatenating the three plans) — both were already implemented, contrary to an initial investigation pass that missed the ticket-creation code path. What was genuinely missing: `change_plan_update` had no state guard, so the plan could still be silently rewritten after the change reached In Progress/Resolved/Closed/Cancelled, after real implementation work had already started against the original plan. It now returns 409 once the change has left New/Awaiting Approval/Approved. No "method" field exists anywhere on the change-task model or its forms; the reported UI inconsistency is presumed to be the CTASK `task_type` ("Type") select, whose forms have been brought in line with the rest of the app's required-field/button conventions — needs confirmation against the original screenshot if this wasn't the intended field. Remaining/open: broader ServiceNow-parity backlog grooming from external reference material was requested but not completed this pass (no PDF text-extraction tooling — `poppler-utils`/`pdftotext` — was available in this environment). Deployed as `serviceops-app:1.27.0`; DB migrated to `20260729_0017`; 79/79 automated tests passed. |
| B-250 | P0 | Verified | Follow-up fixing genuine defects surfaced by real use of [[B-249]]. (1) Root cause of "internal error on change submit": `change_governance.conflict_status` was `VARCHAR(40)`; a real overlapping-change/CI conflict easily produces a message longer than 40 chars (e.g. `"Conflict: CHG0000007 (overlapping change)"`), which PostgreSQL rejected mid-flush as `StringDataRightTruncation`, corrupting the session and cascading into a 500 on the *next* request too (the error page itself failed to render because `current_user.is_active` re-triggered the already-rolled-back session). Widened the column to `VARCHAR(500)` (migration `20260729_0018`, reversible, truncates data on downgrade) plus defensive `[:500]` truncation at the write site. Verified live: submitted a real overlapping change against an existing open CHG/CI and produced a 119-character conflict message that saved and rendered correctly with no server error. (2) Change/incident-create form validation failures (bad dates, missing plan text, invalid team, etc.) previously called `abort(400)`, which threw the user onto Flask's generic error page with all typed text gone and no way back. `ticket_new` now redisplays the same form (HTTP 400) with the validation message flashed and every submitted field/select value preserved, including the required implementation/test/backout plan text fields users are most likely to have spent real time writing — verified live with a bad-dates submission that redisplayed with all three plan fields intact. Also closed unhandled-exception paths in the same route (`int()` on risk score/CI id, missing title/description) that could have produced further 500s on malformed input, and removed a redundant second `tenant_record_or_404` CI lookup by resolving `ci_id` once, up front. (3) Notification bell reported as visually inconsistent with the other topbar icons — it was the only emoji-presentation glyph among monochrome text symbols (◷ ⚙ ? ★☆); added the U+FE0E text-presentation selector plus `font-variant-emoji:text` so it renders as a plain glyph like its neighbors. (4) The three "Locked once the change is submitted…" plan-field notes and the "Priority is calculated from impact and urgency" note were always-visible body text; converted both to the same hover `(i)` info-tip pattern already used elsewhere on the change form (change type/impact/risk score), so they only show on hover/focus. (5) Planned start/end were separate grid cells that could land in different rows depending on how many fields preceded them; wrapped them in a `.field-pair` flex row so they always render side by side as one visual unit. Evidence: 79/79 automated tests pass (migration head bumped to `20260729_0018` in tests). Deployed and verified live: image/worker `serviceops-app:1.27.1`, DB at `20260729_0018`, `change_governance.conflict_status` confirmed 500 chars wide in the running database, end-to-end conflict-creation and validation-redisplay smoke tests both passed against the running app with no errors in the app/worker logs. |
| B-251 | P1 | Verified | Two more reported gaps on the change-task experience. (1) "Why is CTASK not automatically created?" — auto-seeding a first CTASK from the plan on change creation was already implemented (see [[B-249]]/[[B-250]]), but only forward-looking: changes created before that code landed (`CHG0000001-4`, `10`, `25` in the running instance) had zero tasks and no plan text visible anywhere except the read-only governance panel. Migration `20260729_0019` backfills exactly one "Implementation" CTASK for every `change_governance` row with no linked `operational_task`, built from that change's own existing implementation/test/backout plan text and owning team — no data invented, just wiring the plan that was already approved into a visible task record, with a `task_history` entry marking it as backfilled. Downgrade removes only the rows it created (matched via the task_history marker, not a number pattern, since backfilled tasks get renumbered into the normal `CTASK0000NNN` sequence and are otherwise indistinguishable from organically-created ones). A `pg_dump` backup was taken immediately before applying this to the running database. | B-292 | P2 | Verified | Three related requests in one message: (1) the CMDB topology map (`/cmdb/topology`) was "a mess... unable to zoom, unable to select components," wanted progressive disclosure (key points first, detail on zoom) and click-to-inspect (click a switch to see its ports and what's connected, click a server to see which switches it connects to); (2) "resolve the hostname from the IP, and vice versa, because almost all servers have multiple interfaces with a hostname"; (3) the CI-permission grid from [[B-291]] should live in a real GLPI-style Access Control admin section, not a lone CMDB-toolbar link. Clarifying questions confirmed: a genuinely new interactive graph (not an incremental patch), live DNS lookups (not just CMDB cross-referencing), and a real new top-level Administration section. Planned via `EnterPlanMode` with two parallel `Explore` research passes (topology rendering, admin/user-management structure) before writing the plan. **Topology** (`static/cmdb-topology.js` rewritten, still hand-rolled SVG, no external library/CDN): added pan (drag empty canvas) and zoom (wheel + buttons) via an SVG `viewBox` transform; progressive disclosure -- only "backbone" nodes (Switch/Router/Server class, or graph degree >= 2) render initially, everything else starts collapsed behind a "+N more" badge on its nearest expanded neighbor, revealed on click. Clicking a node opens a detail side panel: for a switch/router, a Ports table built from that node's edges (local port / neighbor / neighbor's port, parsed from the existing `CIRelationship.label` format `"Ethernet51 <-> eth0"` established in [[B-289]]); for a server, the same table framed as "connected switches/routers." Kept the existing force-layout physics for initial placement and the CSP-safe `data-graph` attribute pattern from [[B-289]] (no inline `<script>` reintroduced). **DNS resolution**: new `serviceops_core/dns_lookup.py` (`resolve_hostname`/`resolve_ip`), modeled directly on the existing `reverse_dns_lookup()` in `network_discovery.py` -- best-effort, no explicit timeout mutation (deliberately: `socket.setdefaulttimeout` is process-global, not per-call, so scoping it around a single lookup would race with other concurrent requests in the same worker), any failure returns empty rather than raising. New `GET /cmdb/<id>/network-info` route (same `ci_class_read_allowed` permission check as `ci_edit`) resolves the CI's own IP/interfaces to hostnames and vice versa, purely informational -- deliberately not run through the SSRF-focused `_integration_address_allowed` allowlist used for webhook delivery, since nothing here opens an outbound connection to the resolved address, only displays it. Consumed lazily (fetched on node click, not embedded in the initial page load) by both the new topology detail panel and a new "Hostname resolution" line added to the CI edit page's existing "Discovered network details" panel ([[B-287]]). **Access Control admin area**: new `GET /admin/access` hub (`templates/admin_access.html`) and `GET /admin/roles` page (`templates/admin_roles.html`, renders the Git-backed `config/authorization.json` role-action policy read-only via `serviceops_core.security.load_policy()`, plus a link to the existing `/cmdb/permissions` grid) -- both genuinely new routes. Users keeps its existing dedicated `/admin/users` routes, now linked from the new hub instead of `admin_home.html`'s old "Users and access" card. Groups & Teams and Directory (LDAP/OIDC) link into `itil_admin()`'s existing `#team-managers`/`#directory-mapping` sections by anchor rather than being physically extracted into their own routes in this pass -- a deliberate, disclosed scope reduction from the original plan (which called for a full "moved, not duplicated" extraction of those sections into `/admin/groups`/`/admin/directory`): `itil_admin()` is a ~530-line single mixed-concern dispatcher (per [[B-286]]'s own decomposition roadmap, already flagged as a future-phase target) and physically moving its POST action handlers/GET context within the same verification budget as the topology and DNS work was judged too high-risk to safely re-verify end-to-end; the anchor-link approach ships the requested discoverability (a real Access Control hub users can navigate to) without the extraction risk, and is explicitly not a "done" claim on the extraction itself. `base.html`'s `admin_endpoints` list updated to include `admin_access`, `admin_roles`, and (a pre-existing gap) `cmdb_permissions`, so "Administration home" now stays highlighted active on all of them. No schema/migration change. Version bumped to `1.45.0` (minor: new capability). Evidence: full suite 354 passed, 1 skipped (up from 349/1) -- 6 new tests covering `/cmdb/<id>/network-info` (successful resolution with mocked `socket.gethostbyaddr`/`getaddrinfo`, graceful empty-result degradation on `OSError`, and per-class read-permission enforcement matching `ci_edit`) and `/admin/access`/`/admin/roles` (403 for non-admin, 200 with real policy content for admin); two pre-existing tests updated for the intentional `admin_home.html` card rename ("Users and access" -> "Access control"). Deployed and verified live (`serviceops-app:1.45.0`): `/health`/`/ready` green, migration head unchanged at `20260806_0062` (confirmed no drift). Real HTTP-session verification as admin: created a real Switch and Server CI plus a real "Connects to" `CIRelationship` between them, confirmed both nodes and the edge appear in `/cmdb/topology`'s `data-graph` payload; called `/cmdb/<id>/network-info` for the switch (IP `127.0.0.1`) and confirmed a real PTR resolution (`127.0.0.1` -> `localhost`) came back, and for the server (no IP) confirmed graceful empty-result degradation rather than an error; confirmed `/admin/access` renders its four cards and `/admin/roles` renders the real role-action matrix with correct 403/200 gating -- then deleted all verification data (CI relationship and both CIs) from the live database. Not done this pass, disclosed rather than silently dropped: the Groups & Teams / Directory sections were linked, not physically extracted from `itil_admin()` (see above); no automated test coverage for the topology JS itself (pure client-side SVG rendering, consistent with the rest of that renderer -- verified manually via the live HTTP session instead, not in an actual browser with real pointer/wheel events, which remains an unverified area). |

Deliberately left `cmdb_import`/`cmdb_import_netbox` admin-only (bulk/cross-class import stays out of scope for per-row class gating). Version bumped to `1.44.0` (minor: new capability). Migration `20260806_0062` verified with a full fresh-upgrade/downgrade/re-upgrade cycle against real Postgres 16 (columns present after upgrade, absent after downgrade). Deployed and verified live (`serviceops-app:1.44.0`) against the real running deployment: created a real `agent`-role test user, confirmed `POST /cmdb/new` for a brand-new unmanaged class returned 403 by default; used the real admin grid UI to grant that agent `create` (not `update`) on the class; confirmed the same agent's retry succeeded (200, CI created and visible in `/cmdb`) while `GET /cmdb/<id>/edit` on that same CI still returned 403, proving the four permission dimensions are genuinely independent rather than accidentally bundled -- then deleted the test CI, the `CiClassPermission` row, and tombstoned the test user (renamed/deactivated, never hard-deleted, per the established `Audit` FK/append-only-trigger convention). |

| B-289 | P1 | Verified | User shared a screenshot of a real production CMDB search ("bacula") showing the same hostname appearing twice -- once `ci_class=Device`/SNMP-discovered with a real IP, once `ci_class=Server`/no IP, presumably manually imported -- and asked that scan-discovered servers "should not be duplicated and they should be merged with known information," plus a graphical map of "what servers are connected on what ports" derived from switch LLDP data, explicitly excluding VMs ("depend on all the physical devices" -- VM-to-host mapping via oVirt is a separate, deliberately out-of-scope concern for now). Root-caused the duplication: `reconcile_facts_into_cmdb` (`serviceops_core/network_discovery.py`) matched an existing CI by `ip_address` only -- a CI first created manually/via import with no (or a different/stale) IP recorded could never match, so discovery created a second CI at the IP it actually found the device at instead of merging into the existing record. Fixed with a case-insensitive hostname fallback match when the IP lookup misses, and `ip_address` is now refreshed on every match regardless of which lookup found it (previously never touched at all on the update path -- a second, independent bug, since even an IP-matched CI's IP could go stale on a re-scan). New `tools/cmdb_merge_duplicates.py` cleans up duplicates that already exist in a real deployment: dry-run by default (`--confirm` to apply), keeps the `Manual`-sourced record as survivor (oldest by id as tiebreak otherwise), backfills any field the survivor is missing from a losing record, and repoints every FK that can reference a CI (`CIRelationship`, `ServiceOfferingCI`, `ChangeGovernance`, `TaskCI`) before deleting losers -- dropping a repoint instead of crashing if it would violate a unique constraint, since that means the survivor already has the identical link. Added port-level topology detail: the SNMP LLDP walk now also captures the local port (via the `lldpRemLocalPortNum` component already embedded in the walked OID's index, correlated against the already-walked `ifDescr` table for a human name like "Ethernet51"), stored both on the CI's own `lldp_neighbors` attribute (now a 4-column table on the CI page: this device's port / neighbor / neighbor's port / CMDB match, was previously missing the local-port column entirely) and a new `CIRelationship.label` column the topology map renders as text on each edge (e.g. "Ethernet51 ↔ eth0"). `/cmdb/topology` now excludes `ci_class="Virtual Machine"` entirely, matching the explicit "don't worry about VMs" instruction. While touching `cmdb_topology.html`, found and fixed a real pre-existing production bug unrelated to this request: the graph payload was assigned via an inline `<script>window.CMDB_TOPOLOGY_GRAPH = ...</script>` block, silently blocked by this app's own `script-src 'self'` CSP header (no nonce, no `unsafe-inline`) -- the identical class of bug already fixed for `/api/v1/docs` in [[B-276]]. This means the topology map has likely never rendered a populated graph in any CSP-enforcing browser since it shipped; confirmed via a live `curl` that the old inline-script pattern produced no visible error and no working graph. Fixed by moving the payload into a `data-graph` HTML attribute (Jinja's default autoescaping handles the JSON-in-attribute encoding correctly) that the external `cmdb-topology.js` now reads instead. Two migrations: `20260806_0060` (CI class permissions, already documented in [[B-288]]) and `20260806_0061` (`CIRelationship.label`). Version bumped to `1.43.0` (minor: new capability). Evidence: full suite 343 passed, 1 skipped (up from 334/1) -- 9 new tests, including the merge tool's survivor-selection, field-backfill, and FK-reassignment-without-duplicate-violation logic tested directly against real `TaskCI`/`ServiceOfferingCI`/`ChangeGovernance`/`CIRelationship` rows, plus a regression test asserting the old inline-script string is gone from `/cmdb/topology`'s response and a real `data-graph` attribute is present. Both migrations verified with a full fresh-upgrade/downgrade-two-steps/re-upgrade cycle against real Postgres 16. Deployed and verified live (`serviceops-app:1.43.0`) against the real running deployment: created two real duplicate CIs matching the reported shape (same hostname, one Manual/no-IP, one SNMP-discovered/with-IP/with-attributes), dry-ran `tools/cmdb_merge_duplicates` (confirmed it correctly identified the pair and proposed keeping the Manual record without writing anything), then ran it with `--confirm` and confirmed via direct SQL that exactly one row survived with its original `ci_class`/`discovery_source` intact but the missing `vendor`/`ip_address`/`attributes` backfilled from the discovered duplicate; separately confirmed via a real authenticated HTTP session that `/cmdb/topology`'s response contains a real `data-graph` attribute and no inline-script pattern -- then deleted all verification data. Not done this pass, by explicit user instruction: VM-to-oVirt-host topology mapping. |
| B-288 | P2 | Verified | User shared 10 screenshots of GLPI (a different ITSM tool used at their employer): a per-asset-type permission matrix (separate Read/Update/Create/Delete checkboxes per role per asset type -- Computers, Network devices, Printers, etc.), a freeform "draw a line between two assets" impact-relation canvas, a 13-entry custom-named-role list, and an LDAP-synced group list -- asked for "all the features in this screenshot." Two clarifying questions narrowed scope: (1) which pieces to actually build -- user picked only the permission matrix, not the custom-named-roles system (ServiceOps has a fixed 5-tier role model; replicating GLPI's arbitrary-named profiles would be a much bigger authorization redesign) or the freeform relationship-drawing canvas (already overlaps significantly with the existing CI relationships + topology map from [[B-287]]); (2) whether to widen ServiceOps's CMDB create/update/delete routes (currently `@roles("admin")`-only, so agent/manager never reach them) so the grid's write columns could mean something for non-admin roles -- user explicitly declined, keeping create/update/delete admin-only exactly as before. This meant the feature scoped down to **read-visibility filtering only**, not a full CRUD matrix -- a Create/Update/Delete column that can never take effect for agent/manager would have been a footgun, not a feature, so those columns were never built at all rather than shipped inert. Planned via `EnterPlanMode` (research agent mapped the existing role/policy system, a design agent produced the implementation plan, both are the standard workflow now for anything touching the authorization surface). New `CiClassPermission` model (serviceops_models.py) -- opt-in and additive by construction: a CI class (`ConfigurationItem.ci_class` is free text, not a fixed enum like GLPI's literal per-type tables, so the grid's rows are whatever class values are actually in use for a tenant) with zero configured rows is visible to every role that could see CMDB at all before this change; only once an admin adds at least one row for a class does it become "managed," at which point a role needs an explicit read grant or that class's CIs are filtered out of its lists. New DB-backed helper module `serviceops_core/ci_class_policy.py`, deliberately kept separate from `serviceops_core/security.py` (which is file-based/no-DB by design, backing the existing `config/authorization.json` role-action policy this feature is additive to, not a replacement for). Filtering wired into `cmdb()`/`cmdb_export()`/`cmdb_topology()` before pagination/aggregation -- never into `ci_new`/`ci_edit`/`cmdb_import`/relationship routes, which this feature was explicitly scoped to never touch. New admin grid at `/cmdb/permissions` (`templates/cmdb_permissions.html`, `static/ci-permissions.js` -- external file per the `script-src 'self'` CSP lesson from [[B-276]], not inline) with per-row/per-column select-all toggles and an "add a not-yet-used class" input, matching the GLPI screenshot's interaction shape for the one dimension actually built. Migration `20260806_0060`. Version bumped to `1.42.0` (minor: new capability). Evidence: full suite 334 passed, 1 skipped (up from 326/1) -- 8 new tests, the most important being `test_cmdb_unmanaged_ci_class_visible_to_every_role` (the backward-compatibility guarantee: zero rows means zero behavior change) and `test_cmdb_create_edit_unaffected_by_class_permissions` (explicit regression guard that create/update stay ungated by this feature no matter what the grid says). Migration verified with a full fresh-upgrade/downgrade/re-upgrade cycle against real Postgres 16, confirming the table, unique constraint, and index all exist post-upgrade. Deployed and verified live (`serviceops-app:1.42.0`) against the real running deployment: created a real CI of a new class, confirmed it was visible to everyone before management, used the real grid UI to grant only an `agent`-role test user read access, then confirmed via real HTTP sessions that admin's list/export/topology all correctly excluded it while the granted agent's list included it -- then deleted all verification data (CI, permission rows, and the test user, tombstoned rather than hard-deleted since an `audit` FK reference blocked deletion, per the same established convention as [[serviceops-commit-identity]]-adjacent cleanup patterns this session). Not done this pass, by explicit user choice rather than oversight: no Create/Update/Delete dimension (CMDB mutation stays governed solely by the existing role gate); no custom-named-role system; no freeform relationship-drawing canvas. |
| B-287 | P2 | Verified | User sent screenshots from a real scan of an Arista switch: the CI edit page's "Impact analysis" said "Nothing depends on this CI... according to CMDB relationships yet" despite the switch reporting real LLDP neighbors, and the raw `interfaces`/`lldp_neighbors` discovery data appeared as an unreadable Python `str()`'d list-of-dicts blob (`[{'index': '1', 'descr': 'Ethernet1', ...}]`) crammed into a plain text input on the "Additional imported fields" editor -- asked to "review them and optimize our tool to make a map of connected devices each other, and better representation." Root-caused the missing topology link: `reconcile_facts_into_cmdb` (`serviceops_core/network_discovery.py`) only ever matched an LLDP neighbor name against CIs discovered in that *exact same* scan run (`host_to_ci` built fresh per call) -- a single-host scan (the reported case: one switch, target "test") never sees its neighbors' own facts in the same run, so no edge was ever created even when a neighbor had already been scanned and existed in the CMDB from an earlier, separate run; every incremental single-host scan was topologically isolated from every other. Fixed by falling back to a case-insensitive full-CMDB name lookup when the same-run match misses, deliberately restricted to CIs discovery itself created/confirmed (`discovery_source` in SNMP Discovery/Network sweep, never Manual/Import/API) so a hostname collision with an unrelated manually-created CI can never produce a false relationship -- verified explicitly with a dedicated test (`test_reconcile_lldp_neighbor_match_excludes_manually_created_cis`). Root-caused the unreadable blob: the CI form's free-form attribute editor renders every key in `ci.attributes` as a plain text input regardless of type, including structured discovery facts that are lists/dicts, not strings. Moved `sys_descr`/`sys_object_id`/`sys_uptime`/`interfaces`/`lldp_neighbors`/`discovered_via`/`discovered_at` out of the editable rows into a new read-only "Discovered network details" panel (`templates/ci_form.html`) with real Interfaces and LLDP Neighbors tables, the latter linking to the matched CI when the new full-CMDB lookup finds one and showing "Not yet in CMDB" otherwise, plus a pointer to the existing `/cmdb/topology` map. Since those keys no longer round-trip through the editable form, `_ci_attributes_from_form()` (`app.py`) now merges them back in from the CI's existing `attributes` on every save rather than silently dropping them the next time anyone edits an unrelated field -- this was a real latent data-loss bug the read-only-panel change would otherwise have introduced, caught before shipping by writing `test_ci_edit_preserves_discovered_attributes_not_shown_in_the_editable_form` first. No schema change. Version bumped to `1.41.0` (minor: new capability, not just a fix). Evidence: full suite 326 passed, 1 skipped (up from 322/1) -- 4 new tests cover cross-run LLDP matching, the manual-CI exclusion, attribute preservation on save, and the read-only panel actually rendering (with an explicit assertion that the raw `[{'index'` string form never appears in the response body). Deployed and verified live (`serviceops-app:1.41.0`) against the real running deployment with data matching the reported screenshot's actual field values (Arista `sys_descr`/`sys_object_id`, real interface MACs, real LLDP neighbor names): ran two separate `reconcile_facts_into_cmdb` calls simulating two separate scan runs exactly as the user's workflow does, confirmed the second (single-host) run correctly created a "Connects to" relationship to the switch discovered in the first run, confirmed the CI edit page's LLDP table shows a real link for the matched neighbor and "Not yet in CMDB" for the unmatched one, and confirmed `/cmdb/topology` renders both switches -- then deleted all of that verification data back out of the live database. Not done this pass: "Impact analysis" still reports "Nothing depends on this CI" for a pure "Connects to" (peer network link) relationship even after this fix -- that panel's dependency walk is a separate, pre-existing feature answering a different question ("what breaks if this CI goes down," a directional dependency question) and a symmetric network link between peers doesn't answer it the same way a "Depends on"/"Runs on" edge does; not addressed here since it wasn't what was reported broken, but flagged as a possibly-confusing adjacent panel given the docstring's own claim that "Connects to" counts toward impact analysis. |

Performance work: found and fixed a real, previously undiagnosed bug while building this -- `/metrics`'s `HTTP_REQUEST_TOTAL`/`HTTP_REQUEST_DURATION_SECONDS` were in-process `Counter()`/`defaultdict()` globals, so with Gunicorn's 2 worker processes (`GUNICORN_WORKERS`) each scrape only reflected whichever one worker happened to serve a given request -- silently wrong/partial numbers, the same class of bug `RouteRateLimitWindow`'s docstring already warns about for exactly this reason. While wiring "proactively changed according to underlying deployment method," found and fixed a second real bug: `DEPLOYMENT_MODE` was written to `.env` by the installer but never actually forwarded into the container by any of the three Compose files (only `DEPLOYMENT_PROFILE` was) -- so the chart's deployment-mode label always read "unknown" regardless of the real bundled/external/blue-green mode; fixed in `compose.yaml`, `compose.external-db.yaml`, `compose.blue-green.yaml`, and added `DEPLOYMENT_MODE=kubernetes` to the Helm chart's deployment/worker templates (which never had any equivalent). New `tools/stress_test.py` (concurrent authenticated ticket-creation load tool, password via `SERVICEOPS_STRESS_PASSWORD` env var only -- never a CLI argument, matching `tools/admin_recovery.py`'s stdin-only convention since a CLI arg is visible to any other process via `ps`) and `tools/stress_test_cleanup.py` (removes tickets by the tool's title prefix, since incidents have no HTTP delete route unlike changes) -- both added to `./serviceops stress-test`/`stress-test-cleanup`. Deployed and verified live (`serviceops-app:1.40.0` against the real running deployment, not a throwaway stack): `/health`/`/ready` green, migration head correct. Re-verified end-to-end against the live resource-constrained container: `docker stats` showed peak ~294MB RSS (under the 512MB cap) and ~100-103% CPU (at the 1-CPU cap, as expected under contention) throughout the run, `docker inspect` confirmed zero restarts and no OOM kill, and `/health`/`/ready` stayed green immediately afterward. All 959+ synthetic tickets (plus a handful of ad-hoc debug tickets created while diagnosing the tool) were deleted from the live database afterward via `tools/stress_test_cleanup.py` and direct cleanup for the few that predated the tool's title-prefix filter; confirmed the performance chart populates with real non-zero requests/sec and latency values computed from that actual traffic. | B-276 | P2 | Verified | User viewed the new `/api/v1/docs` reference page (added in [[B-275]]'s cycle) and reported it stuck on "Loading…" forever. Root cause: `templates/api_docs.html` fetched `/api/v1/openapi.json` and rendered it from an inline `<script>` block, but the app's own `Content-Security-Policy` header sends `script-src 'self'` with no `'unsafe-inline'` and no nonce — the browser silently refused to execute the inline script, and since the script itself never ran, its own `try/catch` never fired either, so the page gave no visible error, just the static "Loading…" placeholder forever. Not a backend issue: `/api/v1/openapi.json` was returning valid data throughout. Fixed by moving the fetch/render logic to `static/api-docs.js`, referenced via `{{ url_for('static', filename='api-docs.js', v=app_version) }}` (the same external-file + cache-busting-query-string convention every other page in the app already uses), which required passing `app_version=APP_VERSION` into the `api_docs()` route's `render_template` call (previously not needed there). Version bumped to `1.38.1` (patch: bug fix only, no new capability) across `VERSION`, `charts/serviceops/Chart.yaml`, `installer/app.py`, `.env.example`. Evidence: `curl` against the live redeployed stack confirmed `/static/api-docs.js` returns 200 under the unchanged CSP header (external-file scripts satisfy `script-src 'self'`, unlike the removed inline block), and `/api/v1/docs` now references `/static/api-docs.js?v=1.38.1`. Deployed and verified live (`serviceops-app:1.38.1`) against the local test stack; `/health` green. | B-275 | P1 | Verified | User reported three things together against real hardware: only 6 of 17 known hosts found and "just the IP, need this too to be smart"; a request that after a scan the admin can add selected/add all/skip rather than devices landing in the CMDB automatically; and "it run out of memory and crash the app" with nothing in the application log — asked to proactively debug and fix, not just patch symptoms. Investigated the crash first rather than guessing: `docker inspect` showed `OOMKilled=false`/`RestartCount=0` on the live container, so the container itself never got cgroup-killed; found the real root cause by reading what `discover_host` actually does per host — it calls `_snmp_session()` (which constructs a brand-new `SnmpEngine()`) separately for **every** GET/WALK, roughly 9 per host (sys_descr, sys_object_id, sys_name, sys_uptime, an ifDescr walk, an ifPhysAddress walk, an ARP walk, two LLDP walks). `SnmpEngine()` is pysnmp's heaviest object to construct — MIB instrumentation setup, potential `pysmi` MIB compilation on a cold cache — so 40 concurrent sweep threads x ~9 engines/host could trigger thousands of engine constructions within seconds, which is exactly the kind of transient spike that crashes a modestly-provisioned host (explains why nothing showed in the application log: a real memory-pressure kill of a worker process happens below the level Python's own exception handlers ever run — there's nothing to log because the process never got to log it). Fixed by refactoring `discover_host` around a new `_SnmpSession` context manager that constructs exactly **one** `SnmpEngine`/auth/transport/context per host, shared across every GET/WALK for that host inside a single `asyncio.run()` call (down from ~9 event loops + ~9 engines per host to 1 of each) — `snmp_get`/`snmp_walk` (no other callers) were removed as part of this. Verified with real measurements, not just belief: peak RSS for a full real `/24` sweep is 266MB in an isolated fresh container (`resource.getrusage`) and ~470MB observed via `docker stats` against the live deployed stack (up from a ~177MB idle baseline) — bounded and proportional, not runaway. Second fix, for "just the IP, need this too to be smart": `probe_host` now attempts a best-effort reverse-DNS name for bare (non-SNMP) liveness hits via new `reverse_dns_lookup()` — tries the system resolver first, then a direct PTR query (new `dnspython==2.8.0` dependency) against the swept subnet's own gateway address as a nameserver, since many SOHO/business routers (UniFi, pfSense, OPNsense, most enterprise DHCP servers) answer PTR for their own DHCP leases even though Docker's embedded resolver and this user's specific consumer router both didn't in live testing — documented as genuinely best-effort in both code and the manual, not oversold. Third, largest fix: discovery no longer creates a CI on any run, manual or scheduled. New `DiscoveryCandidate` model + migration `20260805_0056` stages every result found; new `GET /cmdb/discovery/<id>/review` page (linked from the discovery list via a "Review N devices" button that only appears when candidates are pending) lists each one with a checkbox, and three actions — "Add selected" / "Add all N" / "Discard all" (`POST .../import` with selected `candidate_id`s or a `select_all` flag, `POST .../discard`) — only ever call `reconcile_facts_into_cmdb` against what was actually chosen; unselected candidates stay pending for a later decision rather than being silently dropped. Deleting a target now also deletes its pending candidates first (required on Postgres — the FK would otherwise reject the delete). Caught a real schema-drift bug while adding this: `DiscoveryCandidate.target_id` was missing `index=True` on the ORM model, so a fresh install's `db.create_all()` (model-driven) and a migrated install's `op.create_index()` (file-driven) would have produced different schemas — `tests/test_governance_migrations_are_reversible` and two sibling reversibility tests failed exactly as designed and caught it before it shipped; fixed by adding the missing `index=True`. Version bumped to `1.38.0` (minor: new capability, though it also contains the memory fix); migration `20260805_0056` verified with a full fresh-upgrade/downgrade/re-upgrade cycle against real Postgres 16, including confirming both indexes exist post-upgrade. Evidence: full suite 302 passed, 1 skipped (up from 298/1) — new tests cover run-stages-without-creating-a-CI, add-selected-only-creates-checked-CIs, add-all-creates-every-candidate, discard-creates-nothing, and review-route `security_administer` gating.
